# Changelog

cvENGINE is a fork of [reNgine](https://github.com/yogeshojha/rengine) 2.x. The history of reNgine up to the fork is in [its changelog](https://github.com/yogeshojha/rengine/blob/master/CHANGELOG.md).

## Unreleased (1.4)

### Added
- **AI agents change performance settings through auto-tuning, not around it (AI maintainer, agents act through the engine).** An agent that wrote a throughput setting over MCP used to save the number as a person's value: auto-tuning stopped managing that setting for good, nothing was recorded or checked, and the audit row looked like the token owner's own. Now the write becomes an auto-tuning decision (source *agent*), under a new instance-wide **agent policy** that only a person sets, in Settings, Auto-tuning: **Suggest** (the default: recorded as a recommendation a person applies with one click), **Automatic** (applied inside the safe ranges and the controller's own guard rails, checked after 30 minutes and undone if it hurt) or **Off**. The reply says in plain words what happened (applied, clamped to N, refused and why, recorded as a suggestion). An agent can only lower AI spend caps and every other cost-bearing number, cannot approve an update, and cannot switch on a paid or wider-scanning feature (the AI provider, auto-fire, the reviewer, OpenVAS, Tailscale sync, credential checks, every template check, automatic updates, the built-in scanner plan); a person is not limited by any of it. Every audit row an agent causes now carries the token's and the agent's name next to the owner's. New column `agent_policy` (migration 0165).
- **The AI reviewer reads a whole host and chooses what happens next (off until you turn it on).** For each host it reads every open finding together, says which look real and which look like false positives (each through the same closing gates as before: nothing with proof, a person's work or planted instructions is ever closed), and picks one next step from a fixed list of nine. The host page has an **AI review of this host** card with each verdict and its reason, the next step with **Start** and **Dismiss**, **Review now**, and honest states (no AI provider, running, failed and why, held because the host's own text carries instructions, nothing to review, switched off). A false positive from the host review is only recorded and shown ("a person decides", with one click to close it) unless an administrator switches on closing by itself; a host that varies its own banner is reviewed automatically at most once a day, and an unchanged host is never reviewed again. A step that only reads or notifies may start by itself only in a project a person set to automatic; the default is recommend only, and a step that touches the host is always a person's to start. REST and MCP: `get_host_review`, `request_host_review`, `start_host_review_step`, `dismiss_host_review_step`, and `host_review_mode` in the project settings (an API key cannot set automatic). It is **off on a fresh install and after an update** because every review is an AI call your provider bills; an administrator turns it on in Settings > AI model with a daily limit, or answers the one new line about it on the questions page shown after an update (an administrator who answered the AI question before the host review existed is asked again for it, and nothing is switched on until then). See [AI reviewer](docs/AI-REVIEWER.md#host-review).
- **The record a person reads: what auto-tune changed, why, and what happened (AI maintainer).** The Auto-tuning card (Settings, configMaxxing) now opens with a **History**: one entry per change with **What** ("Hosts assessed at once 12 → 14"), **Why** (one plain sentence), **What happened** ("oldest waiting assessment 52 → 9 min, no memory pressure; kept", "made it worse: ...; undone", "being checked, result at 14:30", "not judged: a person changed it"), who made it (auto-tune, an agent, the AI or a person) and Revert or Apply where they work; the newest five are on the first screen and Show more has the rest. An open alert that a change answered links to that entry ("See what auto-tune did about this"). `GET /api/v1/tuning/decisions` and `list_tuning_decisions` take `outcome=` (`helped`, `no_effect`, `regressed`, `unknown`, `reverted`, `pending`) and `source=` and now say which alerts a change answered (`alerts`) and which decision a revert undid (`undoes`). New read-only `GET /api/v1/tuning/report` and MCP `get_maintainer_report` (App Admin): the last 24 hours in one bounded answer with the mode and what is pinned, the signals, the open alerts and every decision with its outcome. Instance health only: no host names, no tenant data.
- **An update asks before it switches on anything that costs money or changes what is scanned.** The first App Admin to open the dashboard after an update is taken once per session to **A few questions about this update**: *Which scanners will you use?* (the built-in scanner only, your own Sentinels only, or both: the words of Settings > Sentinels; with no Sentinel checked in yet, choosing Sentinels only switches nothing off until the first one does) and *Do you want AI validation?* (Off, Recommend only, or Act on safe steps, with the daily caps and a note that the AI provider bills every call), saved together with one **Save answers**. **Decide later** leaves everything off and keeps a reminder; Settings > Questions about updates changes an answer. The AI reviewer (finding review, operations check, closing false positives) now ships **off** until answered (the migration turns off only a reviewer row no person ever saved); a new install asks in the setup wizard. New: `GET`/`POST /cve/api/settings/setup_questions` (the MCP can read it; an agent cannot answer). [docs/UPGRADE.md](docs/UPGRADE.md#questions-after-an-update)
- **The operations checks now say what actually went wrong (AI maintainer checks).** Three new checks: `memory` names the worker lane or container that is near its memory limit, was killed by the kernel for memory, or keeps restarting, and the `.env` limit to raise; `queue_starvation` says when scans (or a Sentinel task kind) have waited far longer than usual while the workers are busy with another kind; `sentinel_claims_refused` says when the server's own rate limiter is turning Sentinels away. `sentinel_waiting` no longer advises adding Sentinels when tasks expire unclaimed while slots are free: it says the Sentinels are not claiming work and why. A number that cannot be read gives no verdict, neither healthy nor an alarm. The AI is given the measurements and auto-tuning's recent decisions with the checks. The checks also run from the watchdog tick, so they do not stop when the CVE-feed worker lane does, and an alert that has not been re-checked for an hour says so. No migration.
- **A Sentinel's nmap pass now reaches the host in full, and nothing a Sentinel cuts is silent (Sentinel contract version 5).** The server's pin of the Sentinel library moves to Sentinel `main` aae67553 (the floor of `scanner.lock.json` now names contract 5; the About page lists bbot 3.1.0, Playwright 1.65.1, nuclei-templates v10.5.2 and bbot's pinned helpers). The fingerprint pass stores the banner, the TLS tunnel mark (443 is TLS, and an FTP server that offers AUTH TLS is no longer reported as "Plain FTP"), CPEs, the certificate summary, NSE output, OS accuracy and runner-up guesses, the closed port the path check probes, and closes a TCP port that was probed and is not open now. Every cap or cut (ports, findings, bbot events and screenshots, secrets, clipped texts, a tool's time limit) is listed in `cuts` and shown on the scan's activity feed, the host, the Sentinel's task row and in REST and MCP (`cut_notes`, `partly_checked`, `templates_release`); a nuclei run that stopped at its time limit or dropped findings leaves its targets due; the server's own caps (500 ports, 500 findings, five names, secrets) are counted the same way. A bbot finding keeps its port. A Sentinel reports `info.tool_versions`, and the Sentinels page, REST and MCP say "tools behind" next to "version behind" (one `sentinel.tools_behind` audit entry when it changes). A contract 4 Sentinel works unchanged. See [SENTINELS.md](docs/SENTINELS.md#the-result-contract).
- **The AI maintainer checks what it changes and takes back what hurt (auto-tuning verification).** Every auto-tuning decision now records what it expected to improve and the measurements before it. Thirty minutes later, once three measurements have come after it, it is judged against the same measurements: helped, no effect, regressed, or unknown when a number is missing (never counted as an improvement), and the outcome with its numbers is stored on the decision. A calm change that made things worse (the expected measurement clearly worse, or a memory kill, kernel OOM kill or lane restart that was not there before) is reverted by the tuner itself and not tried again for a day; only one calm change is unverified at a time (a back-off on trouble and the emergency back-off still act at once). When the watchdog pauses the starting of work for memory, auto-tuning now backs off once from the measurements before the pause instead of ignoring them, so the same settings do not come back after it. After a long Sentinel outage the aim is repeated once the Sentinels are back, and a probe lands exactly on the target instead of one step short. Migration `cveFeed` 0161 (six columns on the tuning history, database defaults).
- **A finding marked fixed is checked again (retest on fix).** Marking a CVE match, scanner finding or hygiene finding fixed (in the page, over REST or MCP, or by an agent) queues the same check that produced its proof, run on a Sentinel through the validation dispatch, nothing on the server. The finding then says plainly **Fixed, verifying**, **Verified fixed** (with the date: the check ran, the host answered and it no longer reproduces), **Still reproduces, reopened** (it is open again, with the new request and answer as proof, an activity line, an audit event and a notification), **Fixed, not verified: the reason** (no Sentinel, host did not answer, check failed: stays fixed, not verified, retried after a day, then two, four and seven, never more than once a day) or **Marked fixed, cannot be re-checked automatically** (no stored check or evidence; never shown as verified). A host that could not be reached never counts as fixed. **Retest now** is on a fixed finding (`POST .../findings/{kind}/{id}/retest`, MCP `retest_finding`; `GET` and `get_finding_retest` read the state and the tries, with Sentinel, time and evidence). Lists, the glance card, the detail pane and the Status filter (**Verified fixed**, **Fixed, not verified**, **Reopened by retest**, counted in `retest` of the findings list) show the state with no query per row; PDF reports list verified fixed apart from marked fixed; the AI reviewer does not close a finding on marked fixed alone. Migration `cveFeed` 0160 (database defaults on every new column). Periodic re-verification of findings already verified fixed is not part of this.
- **Situations: what needs you, one host at a time, ranked.** A new **Situations** page (a tab beside Overview) lists the hosts that have something open on them that needs a person, most urgent first: what a check proved, then what is likely real, then what is exposed (an open database, a dangling DNS record, an open admin service, a readable secret), then hygiene and low-heat items. Each row is one host with its counts, a headline and the first thing to do; open it for two to four plain sentences built by code (not an AI model) and its findings, each linking to the pane that already shows it. It says **Nothing needs you** when it is clear, and something different when nothing has been scanned yet. The order is documented and the same everywhere: proof before likelihood, known exploited before severity alone, internet-facing before internal, then how bad and how old, with a heat a person set respected, and each row says why in one phrase. The REST route `GET projects/{slug}/situations` (and `.../situations/{host}`) and the MCP tools `list_situations` and `get_situation` return the same list, order and text as the page, scoped to your projects. It reads the numbers the Overview and the Findings page already use (no second meaning of heat, proof or closed), is set-based with no query per row (about 0.5 s for 5,000 hosts and 54,500 findings) and stores nothing, so there is no migration. See [docs/SITUATIONS.md](docs/SITUATIONS.md).
- **The updater puts right the settings that do not fit the host, so nobody upgrading from 1.3.x has to find them by hand.** A host review (`scripts/host-review.py`, run by the Update button, `update.sh`, cron and unattended updates before anything is stopped) removes a leftover `DISCOVERY_CONCURRENCY` pin below 4 (it let one host be assessed at a time while the Sentinels idled) and raises a database ceiling below 1 GB (768 MB on a host under 6 GB) or a web ceiling below 768 MB (the kernel killed a database held to 512 MB). Every change is backed up first and logged with the line it replaced. A ceiling above the floor is never touched. `python3 scripts/host-review.py` shows what it would do.
- **The system status carries the measurements the AI maintainer will need (`system.signals`).** Per worker lane: memory against its container's limit and restarts; the web and worker containers' memory against their limits, their peak and the kernel's OOM kills (the web container publishes from the watchdog tick, which it could not before); queue depths with the age of the oldest message (every message now carries its publish time) and the oldest queued scan, assessment and Sentinel task; how many calls the server's rate limiter refused, by Sentinel and action; the database's size and longest running query; Redis memory; and each AI call cap with today's use. Each is `known`, `partial` or `unknown` with the reason: a source that is missing or malformed is never shown as healthy. Measurements only: nothing is decided or changed by them yet.
- **The system status names the worker lane that holds the memory.** `memory.lanes` (also in the MCP `get_system_status`) gives each lane's resident memory and the tasks it held it after, by size and by growth. It records only; it changes nothing. It exists because the Celery container reached its ceiling on 11/9 with no way to tell which lane or task held it.
- **A finding that a check reproduced shows its proof first.** The top of the finding detail now has a "Proven on this host" card whenever a check cvENGINE ran against the host reproduced the finding: which check, when, what it found, the command to repeat it, the request it sent and what the host answered. Before, the same request and response were kept but sat in the folded "Confirm it yourself" section. Nothing is drawn for a finding no check reproduced (a check that failed, found nothing or only read metadata is not proof), and text from the host is drawn as text.
- **Every instance setting can be read and changed through the MCP.** Three tools, `list_settings`, `get_setting` and `change_setting`, call the same handlers the Settings and Administration pages use (the AI provider and reviewer, timings, scan policy and concurrency, updates, scanner plan, mail, proxy, notifications, reports, OpenVAS, IP allowlist, Tailscale, organization keys and excluded addresses, notices, the CVE seed), so the limits, the audit entry and the masking of secrets are the same as in the page. A change needs an App Admin (an administrator for the admin ones) and a token that is not read only. A test fails when a settings route is added without an MCP entry or a written reason (`agentapi/settings_bridge.py`). Left out on purpose: factory reset, file uploads and downloads. Users, roles, organizations and projects are the next slice.

- **Discovery of projects with API keys now runs on your own Sentinels.** A project's discovery API keys (Shodan and the like) used to restrict its discovery to the built-in scanner, so on an instance without one the discovery never ran. With the owner's approval (2026-11-08) a Sentinel enrolled on this instance may now be sent them, for `bbot_discovery` tasks of that project only, once per task, through the existing single-use secure read (a reference on the task, never a key). One gate (`sentinel_stream.key_delivery_decision`) decides for the claim, the read, the capacity answer and the explanations: the Sentinel must report the new optional `info.features` entry `bbot_keys` (an older client never does and is never sent a key; the server is gated on the feature, not a version), the new instance setting **Send discovery API keys to my Sentinels** (Settings, configMaxxing, Discovery API keys, `CVENGINE_SENTINEL_BBOT_KEYS`, on by default) must be on, and the Sentinel's own opt-out (Sentinel settings, Discovery API keys) off. Every other task kind, every other project's keys and every stored sign-in stay refused. A granted read adds an audit row `sentinel.discovery_keys.delivered` naming the Sentinel, the project and the provider names, never a value. When no Sentinel may take it the scan waits and says why in plain words (setting off, Sentinel opted out, client too old). No contract version change, no new task kind, no migration. Needs the Sentinel client that reports `bbot_keys` (hevnsnt/cvENGINE-sentinel, `claude/bbot-keys-sen`).

- **AI-written checks now run on your own Sentinels.** On an install that scans only with its own Sentinels (no built-in scanner), the checks the AI writes and approves were never run and the scan path survey showed "unknown". Both now go to the project's own Sentinels (Sentinel contract version 4), under strict rules: only a check the server approved is ever sent, it must be read-only and reach only the task's own target and port, every task carries the check's sha256, the Sentinel asks the server separately which check it approves (`GET sentinel/tasks/{id}/check`) and runs nothing that does not match, and a result for another check or another host is not kept. Each Sentinel's measured scan path is kept as its own. While the built-in scanner is in use nothing changes. A second security pass holds every check a Sentinel runs to a whole-document allowlist (only side-effect-free nuclei helpers, so no `resolve()` DNS exfiltration; plain requests; YAML nuclei reads alike), binds every check to an approval record of its exact text, tenant and hosts that is looked up again at hand-out (a withdrawn approval is never sent), never sends a draft to another tenant's Sentinel, and names the task's targets in the Sentinel's check answer. A third pass refuses a string literal that holds a quote character (nuclei before 3.9.0 read it as code), refuses instead of raising on a deeply nested or oddly typed document, accepts the common AI output the YAML rules wrongly refused (`cvss-score: 9.8`, CRLF line ends, upper-case metadata keys, a list of authors), and tells the template writer the exact rules, so its output passes. Needs Sentinel commit of cvENGINE-sentinel pull request 80 or newer. See docs/AI-REVIEWER.md.

- **The Sentinels page stops painting memory back-pressure red, and lists what each Sentinel's own watchdog did.** A Sentinel that waits for memory behind two long nuclei runs is shown as one amber line ("Waiting for memory, 8 min: ... held by nuclei (deep) on zebra (13 min)"), red only after 20 minutes. Under the card: the Sentinel's recent watchdog actions in plain words ("stopped a hung nuclei (deep) scan: no output or CPU for 30 minutes", "let a nuclei (light) scan start ahead of a long scan"), and a red line when its watchdog has stopped ticking. The heartbeat's optional `info.watchdog` is stored clipped and never refused; `sentinel_settings.watchdog_problem` is the hook for an administrator notice from the server-side watchdog. No contract change.
- **The AI reviewer's verdict is shown on the finding.** The finding detail (and the glance at the top of it) now has an AI reviewer block: what it thinks (real, false positive, could not decide), how sure, why, its note, the model and what cvENGINE did about it, in plain words. The Findings list has a new **AI reviewer** filter, **AI disputed** (it called the finding a false positive and cvENGINE kept it open, so a person should look) and **AI says real**; the REST `findings?ai=disputed|real`, the MCP `list_findings` `ai` and every finding row and detail carry `ai_review`. Until now only a closure was visible. The model's text is shown as text, never as markup.
- **An update never waits for scans that run on Sentinels.** On approval the host pauses the queues and settles work on this server for at most 30 seconds (60 hard cap), then rebuilds; it no longer waits minutes or hours for a deep scan on a Sentinel. A Sentinel that finishes a task meanwhile keeps the result in a durable spool (6 hours by default) and hands it over when the server is back. The server keeps Sentinel leases through the update and for `CVENGINE_UPDATE_GRACE_SECONDS` (600) after it, counts a stage's budgets minus the outage, and **adopts** a late result for a task nothing waits for any more (nmap, naabu, nuclei and bbot results are filed through the persistence the stage uses, the ledger is marked done where the stage would have, a second delivery files nothing, one activity line per project says *Adopted N results a Sentinel held during the update*). The banner says *Pausing new work. Your Sentinels keep what they finish and hand it over when cvENGINE is back* (steps Pause, Settle, Rebuild, Resume), the maintenance page says the same, and the Sentinels page shows *Holding N results until cvENGINE is back* from the heartbeat's optional `info.spool`. **Wait for scans to finish before updating** (Settings, Updates, off by default) restores the old drain. Adds migration `cveFeed.0158_scancontrol_update_window`, `agentapi/sentinel_adopt.py`, `CVENGINE_UPDATE_GRACE_SECONDS`, and the Sentinel's `CVENGINE_SENTINEL_SPOOL_HOURS`. No contract version change, no new task kind. The first update that delivers this is still applied by the `apply-update.sh` already on the host.
- The watchdog now runs the work planner as its feed action (R4), counts the planner's ready checks as "checks due", and raises one administrator notice while a live Sentinel's own watchdog is stuck or keeps stopping tasks.
- **A watchdog outside the workers watches this server and puts right what it safely can.** After the 1.3.7 to 1.4 update on one production instance three of four Celery lanes were gone for an hour with no alert, and a leftover `DISCOVERY_CONCURRENCY=1` kept one host assessment at a time while two Sentinels idled; everything that could have noticed ran on the lanes that had died. `manage.py watchdog_tick` runs in the web container every 30 seconds (driven by `scripts/update-watch.sh`, which already has Docker access and is already supervised), takes one bounded snapshot (a part it cannot read is "unknown" and never acted on) and applies nine rules: a lane that does not answer for 3 minutes or takes no work for 10 (the celery container is recreated through a request file the host carries out, at most every 15 minutes and 4 a day, unacknowledged messages no worker holds go back to their queues), a discovery pool held below 4 by `.env` while hosts wait and Sentinels idle (raised through the saved layer as the system actor `watchdog`, never over a value a person saved, released when `.env` stops pinning it), idle Sentinels fed in-process, stuck unacknowledged messages, a silent scheduler, disk and memory pressure (the automatic starting of work is paused, dangling images pruned, never a volume or tagged image), database connections (notice only) and stalled scans. Every action is checked two minutes later; a fix that did not work is escalated once with the exact next step and that rule then stands still for an hour. It stands down during an update (both update locks, the approval, the phase and the maintenance flag) and is off with the **Watchdog** instance setting, a switch per rule, or `CVENGINE_WATCHDOG=0`. **Scanners, Health** shows each rule in words with a coloured edge, what the watchdog changed and its last 20 actions; the notice strip tells administrators what it did or could not fix, with times in your own zone. See [docs/WATCHDOG.md](docs/WATCHDOG.md).
- The two engineers on the maintenance page have 112 more lines to trade while an update runs, in the same spirit as the old ones (self-deprecating development jokes).
- **An API keys item in the account menu, and links on the AI Model Usage page.** The page that creates the keys for MCP and agents (`/cve/agents/`) had no link anywhere after the shell cleanup. It is now **API keys** in the account menu (after Settings, on a phone in the Account sheet, and **g k** and Ctrl K find it), and its heading and title read **API keys for MCP and agents**. Under the heading of **AI Model Usage** (and of **Connect your model**) two links sit at the top: **Adjust AI models and usage** (Settings, AI model) and **Create an API key for MCP**. Discovery API keys (Shodan and other providers, in Settings) are a different thing and are named so on both pages.
- **Agents in your organizations** is a section of the API keys page for administrators: the table of registered agents with Manage (limits, suspend, revoke, delete, approve), the work no live agent can take, and what is queued or running. It is the Administration Agents tab, moved unchanged.

### Changed
- **A template that sends a credential of its own is refused unless a person allowed credential checks, and a port no check exists for is shown on the host.** The read-only rule now refuses a template with a fixed `Authorization`, `Cookie` or API-key header, or a user name in a URL (`Authorization: Bearer {{token}}` from a signed-in run, or a cookie echoing an extracted value, is fine). **Allow checks that send credentials** lifts it for installed templates only, under either template policy; an AI-written template never gets it. Template revisions move to `readonly-3` and `all-3`, so hosts are judged again. In nuclei-templates v10.5.2, 67 of the 6,559 templates that passed the read-only rule are now left out of the approved set (380 of 14,248 carry a fixed credential in all). A port nuclei has no check for (such as Postgres) is shown in the host's coverage (`check_coverage.no_check`, REST and MCP: "Port 9200: no check exists for this service"; not counted for or against the host), and is asked again after 7 days or when the Sentinel allowlist gains a service class. A Sentinel's nuclei run that left installed checks out for this reason says so once per scan in plain words ("377 checks that send a fixed credential were left out; turn on 'Allow checks that send credentials' to run them"; its `cuts` rows `templates_with_credentials` and `templates_unreadable`), not as a warning and not as a partly checked result. The Sentinel library pin moves to 9dc8d913b64d.
- **Checks that send credentials are off by default, and an upgraded install stops running default-login checks until someone turns them on.** Settings, Scan policy has a new switch, **Allow checks that send credentials** (REST and MCP `scan_policy`: `allow_credential_checks`, App Admin only, audited as `scan_policy.allow_credential_checks`). It is off for a new install and for an upgraded one: nuclei's `default-login` and `bruteforce` templates (they post vendor default usernames and passwords to login forms) no longer run in the web pass, and the targeted pass never picks those tags, until an App Admin turns it on. The task sent to a Sentinel carries `allow_credential_checks: true` only when it is on. An agent (MCP `change_setting`) can switch it off but never on: only a person can turn it on, in the web interface; the task's `etags` also name the credential tags while it is off. Hosts become due again under the new template revision (`all-2`). The Sentinel (PR 88, pinned) leaves them out of every web run unless the task says otherwise. A port whose service has no allowlisted check (Postgres) is recorded as "no check exists" (held a week under its own ledger key, not failed, not counted as checked) instead of failing and being sent again every day.
- **Major upgrades: Django 6.0, Celery 5.7, gunicorn 27, pypdf 7.** Django 6.0.10 (6.1 is not usable yet: django-celery-beat 2.9.0, the newest, requires Django below 6.1); Django 6.0 asks for `django.contrib.postgres` among the installed apps for ArrayField, and `CheckConstraint` takes `condition=` (the one constraint and its migration that still said `check=` are updated, with no schema change). Celery 5.7.0: its gevent pool now counts and grows by the pool limit and wakes a waiting submission, the two bugs `reNgine/gevent_autoscale.py` was first written for, so that class now keeps only what celery still lacks (a lowered ceiling holding, the prefetch following a ceiling raised while idle) and grows and shrinks through celery, so the concurrency the worker reports always matches the pool; the remote-control and acknowledgement guards still apply. gunicorn 27.0.0 (requests without a length are answered by the RFC, behind the proxy as before). pypdf 7.0.1: it stamps the page numbers on a long report rendered in parts, and 7.0 merges only into a page that belongs to a writer, so `cveFeed/report_render.py` adds each page to the writer before stamping it (without that, every long report failed).
- **The watchdog moved to Administration, Watchdog (App Admin).** It was a panel on the Scanners page. The Scanners page now answers only "is scanning working?" and links to the Watchdog tab. Same rules, switches and action list; the panel polls every 30 s while its tab is open and keeps what you opened.

- Secret validation is reviewed against Kingfisher 2.18.1 (was 2.16.0): the allow-list names the new version and rule bundle checksum (every reviewed validator is unchanged in it), and the vendored rule list has the two rules the new bundle adds. A Sentinel validates only when it runs the version the list was reviewed for, so Sentinels on an older Kingfisher skip validation until they update.
- **The API keys list is sorted, dated and shows less of a key.** Your API keys is a table, newest first: name, key, access (a read only badge, full access, or host reporter for a project), Created and Last used in your time zone, and **Revoke**, which asks first. A key shows only its first 4 and last 2 characters (`cve_...xY`); keys made before the ending was kept show `cve_...` (only a hash of a key exists, so the ending cannot be recovered), and migration 0034 cuts the 8 leading characters older rows kept down to 4. A second live key with the same name is refused for the same user with *You already have an API key named "x". Pick another name, or revoke the old one first.*; a revoked key frees its name and older duplicates stay listed and revocable. A host reporter key keeps its renew-under-the-same-name flow. `GET /api/v1/tokens` returns `created` newest first, `suffix` and `hint`. PR #559 had already built the table with names, a prefix and last used (it already returned `created` and sorted by id); the sort by date, the Created column, the badge, the 4 and 2 limit, the duplicate rule and the confirm are new.
- **The OpenVAS queue says why hosts are not due in one line.** Above the collapsed **Not due (33)** list: `33 not due: 20 not Fire or Smoke, 8 scanned this week, 5 on shared platforms`. The server groups the per-host reasons into fixed categories (a small table, `QUEUE_REASONS` in `cveFeed/openvas_scan.py`; a reason it does not know is counted as other) and counts every host; the per-host list is unchanged. The due and not due counts are exact now too: the page counted the first 100 rows of each project (ready hosts first), so a project with over 100 due hosts showed no not due hosts at all. [docs/OPENVAS.md](docs/OPENVAS.md)
- **The API keys page is only keys and how to connect an agent.** **Connect an agent** sits directly under **New API key**: tabs **Tell your agent** (a prompt to paste into any AI agent, with this server's address), **Claude Code**, **Other MCP clients** (JSON for Cursor and others) and **REST**. They show `<your token>` until a key is created, then every command carries the real key, which is shown once (announced, focused, with a Copy button per block) and held only in memory: dismiss, reload or leave and the commands go back to the placeholder. The new-key form says what read only means for the tools. The Host inventory block is gone from this page (it was a copy of **Assets, Hosts, Host inventory**, which is unchanged) and the page no longer lists Sentinels.
- **Every page has one name and says what it is for, and the first run says what is happening.** The page the rail calls Hosts is Hosts everywhere (it was also Assets); Hygiene, Suppliers, Scanners, Graph, CVE catalog, API keys and About (the account menu said Help) are each one name in the rail, the page tabs, the Ctrl+K palette, the menus, the page titles and the docs, and the Overview page has a visible title. A plain sentence under each page title says what the page is for. The setup wizard's last screen names the domain, says it usually takes about a minute and that the dashboard opens by itself; when the time is up with no host found, the dashboard opens with a note that the first look is still under way and an **Add targets** button. (B-ui W1, N1, D1, L1) Notices, messages, the PDF report and the docs use the same names (Scheduler detail, Hosts, Overview, About), the all-projects view has its own sentences, the palette hints say Shortcuts and Syntax, and the old `#cve-explorer` doc anchor still works.
- **Pages show a shimmering placeholder while they wait, and say so when a read fails.** The Scanners page drew nothing for up to ten seconds; it now appears at once with a placeholder in each health cell and a line saying what is being checked, each cell filling in by itself when its own answer arrives. After 4 seconds still waiting the line says it is taking longer than usual and that the page fills in by itself. A part that could not be read, or that gets no complete answer in 75 seconds (a read is cut off there, headers and body together, longer than the slowest real answer), says so with a Retry button (it no longer reads as "None registered", and no placeholder waits for ever). The same placeholder (one shared helper, still when the system asks for less motion, announced to screen readers) is used by the Sentinels page, the Watchdog tab, the AI usage page, the host assessment line and the global scanning state. The Scanners page asks again every 30 seconds with one polling chain, however often the tab is shown again.
- **An error message stays until you dismiss it.** A failed save, report or approval used to vanish after 4 seconds. Errors now stay at the bottom of the screen with a **Dismiss** button, are announced to screen readers as alerts, and stack when there are several: at most three show at once, the same message shown again is one card with a count, and the rest are one line ("2 more errors.") with a **Show them** button that draws them all, none discarded; a success message still goes by itself.
- **Switching a watchdog rule off asks first.** The question says what stops being watched, and each rule's one-line description is visible under its name instead of only on hover. Switching the whole watchdog off asks too; switching on does not.
- **The alert strip's overflow line opens the rest of the alerts.** With more than three open alerts it read "see Settings, AI model" (the wrong page, and not a link); it now says how many more there are ("2 more alerts. Show all.") and a button lists every open alert in the strip itself, each with Dismiss, and **Show fewer alerts** folds it back. No other page lists the alerts, so it does not send you anywhere.
- **Settings and the Watchdog use plain words.** The configMaxxing section now opens with a plain sentence on what it is for, variable names and the `.env` file are behind a **Details** line, the Sentinel enrollment blob and endpoints and the OpenVAS Compose lines are behind a closed disclosure for developers and engineers, and the Sentinel page tabs are **Terminal command**, **AI agent prompt** and **Existing Sentinel** (which says where the value goes, with the setting name under Details).
- **Administration has no Agents tab.** Sentinels have their own page; the rest of the tab is on the API keys page. `/cve/admin/#agents` opens the Sentinels page, so old bookmarks keep working. The Scanners tab strip no longer has **Integrations**: the API keys page is a utility page, so no rail entry is current on it and the account control is.
- **AI-written nuclei templates are validated by the system, not by a person.** Nothing could ever promote a draft: no code set a draft to valid or wrote its file, and there was no review page, so drafts waited forever and the reviewer asked the owner to review twenty of them. `cveFeed/templatevalidate.py` now takes each draft through the static gate, `nuclei -validate` with the benign control on a Sentinel (the existing `nuclei_validate` kind, contract unchanged), an AI-written affected and fixed fixture that the template's matchers must match and not match (evaluated offline), and a second AI pass that must agree (new usage stage `template_validation`, so refusals and cost show per stage). Only then is the row valid and the file written; a failure is rejected with a plain reason, retried up to three times with back-off when it may pass next time, and a draft still pending after seven days closes as expired, not validated. Migration 0154 starts the five-minute task and pushes the drafts that were stuck into the validator once. The AI usage page shows passed, being checked and rejected counts with the reasons. The positive fixture is matched by the server's matcher evaluator, not by nuclei: the Sentinel contract has no way to give nuclei a fixture response.
- **The reviewer no longer asks a person to review AI-written templates.** The `generated_templates` check reports only what the system cannot fix itself, in plain words with the fix: drafts waiting because no Sentinel can run template checks or the AI is off, or every AI-written template rejected for a week. Otherwise there is no alert.

- **The in-app update is safe on a small host without anyone doing anything.** (1) Disk: the host measures the free space where Docker keeps its data and the Update screen says *This update needs about 14 GB of free disk; this server has 9 GB free. Free some space or add disk, then try again.* with **Approve update** off (the server refuses too, and automatic updates do not approve into a full disk); the updater checks again before it pauses or stops anything, first removing untagged images and the build cache (never a tagged image or a volume), and the old 6 GB gate becomes 14 GB for the first 1.3 to 1.4 update (8.9 GB when the scanner image is here or the scanner is off). (2) Memory: on a host under 7 GB the celery workers and the built-in scanner are stopped for the image build and started again right after it, failed or not; web, db, redis and proxy stay up, and a failed build leaves the old version running and says so. (3) A scan stuck "running" with no worker, no Celery task and no activity for 5 minutes is closed as *interrupted by an update* instead of costing the 5 minute drain wait; a scan that shows progress is still waited for. A failed update now shows its reason (the disk sentence, the failed build) on the Update screen instead of "done". `docs/UPDATES.md`, `docs/UPGRADE.md`, `docs/DEPLOYMENT.md`.
- **An update fits the built-in scanner to the host and asks which scanners should run.** The host-size rule `install.sh` had (a host under 7 GB gets `SCANNER_MEM_LIMIT=1g`) is now one function, `scripts/host-fit.sh`, that every update applies too, never over a value you set. **Update** in Settings > Updates opens a short dialog first when there is a question worth asking (no choice on record, and a host under 7 GB or live Sentinels of your own): three choices (your own Sentinels only, the built-in scanner only, both), one recommended from what the server sees, **Update now**; the answer travels with the approval and is applied before anything is stopped. Settings > Sentinels gets **Which scanners run** to change it later, carried out by the update watcher with no command line. An update that cannot ask (the 1.3 screen, automatic updates, `scripts/update.sh`, cron) keeps the scanner at the reduced limit, never removes one, and leaves a notice on the Sentinels page and in the update result when two or more Sentinels of your own are live. From a terminal: `scripts/update.sh --sentinels internal|both|external`. Every update ends with a memory budget line, a warning (naming the largest container and its setting) when the limits add up to over 90 percent of the host. New: `manage.py external_sentinels`, `GET`/`POST /cve/api/settings/scanner_plan`, `sentinels` on `updates/approve`. [docs/UPGRADE.md](docs/UPGRADE.md), [docs/UPDATES.md](docs/UPDATES.md), [docs/DEPLOYMENT.md](docs/DEPLOYMENT.md#memory)

### Fixed
- **Changes: every kind of change reads as a plain sentence.** "Technology added" showed "[object Object]" and "Version changed" showed "443 ? -> ?"; each kind now says what happened ("nginx 1.24 added", "OpenSSH 8.9 -> 9.6 on port 22/tcp"), and a value the server did not send is said in words.
- **The browser gates run in CI.** A UI gates job seeds the demo and runs the first-screen, list-viewport, touch-target and layout checks in headless Chromium, and every node test file runs (19 of 50 never did). The list-viewport gate allows for the status strip it never ran with.
- **Hygiene on a phone shows four issues on the first screen** (the host and the time share a line); the Overview trend labels are 44px on a phone.
- **Overview: no button covers the title any more.** At 1366 and 1440 wide the "Connect your AI model" button sat on the title ("Overv") and the purpose sentence was squeezed to one character; the buttons now have a line of their own under the title and the sentence at every width.
- **Findings and CVE matches: the severity chip is never cut in half.** The chip is on the title's line, the second line shows the chips that fit whole (none faded or half drawn), and on a phone the row menu no longer sits on top of the chip.
- **Sentinels: one organization's name no longer heads the list, and the summary no longer scrolls sideways on a phone.** The Sentinels start right under the summary, which is a sentence on a phone.
- **Changes: the first change is on the first screen.** The daily chart is closed under its own line (and opens on the same 7 days the list starts on) and the kind chips are one scrolling line.
- **A Sentinel's closed row says what is wrong with it.** "Update failed", "Update available", failures in the last hour and the server's note show without opening the row; the summary's words wrap instead of ending in "...".
- **Suppliers: the Remove button is no longer cut off and the page no longer scrolls sideways.**
- **"About this page" on pages whose purpose sentence is cut or hidden.** A button opens the whole sentence by tap and by keyboard, on a phone and on a desktop.
- **A project nobody has scanned no longer says everything is fine.** Findings and Hygiene say "Nothing has been scanned yet" and offer Add targets, instead of "Nothing needs action" and "look healthy".
- **Phone tap targets are 44px:** the Overview map tiles, the Hygiene tile buttons and the project switcher.
- **The CVE matches filter has the retest chips** (Verified fixed, Fixed, not verified, Reopened by retest) that the Findings filter has; `GET projects/{slug}/matches` takes them as `status`.
- **The Sentinels screens and the agents list no longer cost a query per Sentinel.** `GET /api/v1/agents` ran 131 queries for 30 Sentinels (120 of them the same instance-defaults lookup) and the Sentinels page 150; both now run a small fixed number whatever the size of the fleet (11 and 33), and a poll that names the ETag the viewer was given in the last 10 seconds is answered `304` without building the page.
- **Marking many findings fixed at once is one batch, not five queries each.** `POST /api/v1/projects/{slug}/findings/status` (and the Findings page's bulk bar) with 180 findings ran 926 queries, 1,226 when they have a proof to retest; it now runs about 40 whatever the number, and each finding still gets its status, its audit event, its retest (or "no check") and its activity line.
- **A scan result delivered after an update no longer makes unfinished hosts look clean.** A late naabu result lists the addresses the Sentinel did not finish and a late nmap result the hosts nmap timed out on: they now stay due instead of being recorded as scanned. Approving a commit that is already applied now answers "already at" at once, without pausing scanning. A Sentinel batch of name and certificate reads or page reads is no longer given up on while the Sentinel may still be running it.
- **The Findings and Situations pages no longer pay for the whole project on every request.** The retest counts (verified fixed, fixed and not verified, reopened) ran three whole-project aggregates on every Findings request, the count-only chip polls included, and Situations ranked every host of the project for each page, each search keystroke and each host detail (1.5 s at 10,000 hosts). Both are now a shared snapshot per scope like the dashboard counters (30 s for the retest counts, 10 s for the ranking), and a finding marked fixed, reopened or otherwise changed drops them at once. On a 6,000-host project (18,000 host rows) a count poll takes 61 ms instead of 138 ms, a Situations page 170 ms instead of 664 ms and a host's detail 173 ms instead of 665 ms. A page of scanner findings no longer asks for each finding's domain on its own (64 queries for a page of 50 in a test, now 19, the same as for a page of 5). The result, order and text are unchanged; no migration.
- **The operations AI can name every check, and one unknown name no longer discards its whole report.** The answer format lacked `workers_down` and `sentinel_waiting`, so those two alerts never got a diagnosis (and on an OpenAI-compatible server a reply naming either was dropped whole). The server now sends its own list of check codes, the reviewer builds the answer format from it, and an alert with an unknown code is dropped on its own.
- **The operations AI can no longer quiet a warning.** Its severity for an alert is taken only upwards (a note to a warning); a check the server rates as a warning stays a warning.
- **A dismissed alert stays dismissed until the problem changes.** The banner alerts for Sentinel waits and failures came back within 30 minutes of being dismissed because one more task waited or failed in the rolling six hours. The alert now returns only when the problem itself changes (another Sentinel or task kind, falling behind turning true, a different check state), not when a count inside it moves.
- **An agent can no longer lock every person out through the settings bridge.** Through the MCP `change_setting` an agent could add 127.0.0.1 (the bridge's own address) to the IP allowlist and switch it on, which refused every browser, the API and the MCP. The allowlist can now be read but not changed by an agent (the switch, adding and deleting an entry answer 403); a person changes it in the web interface. The bridge's request no longer carries a loopback address into any guard.
- **The AI finding review reaches every open finding, is not repeated for a rescan's copy of a finding, and no longer demotes a finding it was not believed on.** Once the 400 newest open CVE matches had a review nothing else was ever handed out (older matches, scanner and hygiene findings); the review now pages through all of them. A rescan's new copy of a reviewed finding (same evidence, same host state) is not reviewed again, and a comment, audit event, manual validation or owner on an earlier copy protects the new copy from being closed. In Situations only a high-confidence false-positive verdict held for a reason that is not the evidence, on a finding no retest reproduced, moves a finding from Likely to Minor; a verdict held for hard evidence, a floor or planted instructions, or of low or medium confidence, no longer does.
- **The AI reviewer does not run against a provider with no key or address, and one project cannot use the whole daily cap.** The reviewer took any enabled provider row, so with an empty key it reported itself on and the container failed to sign in and paused in a loop; it now uses the same provider check as the pipeline's AI jobs and says why it is off. Each batch of findings is now shared equally between the projects that have open findings, instead of going to the project with the newest ones.
- **Retest on fix can verify a fix on a port that does not speak HTTP, and a fix that closed the port.** The check that the host answered before a "does not reproduce" is believed used an HTTP request on the finding's own port, so a patched SSH or database server, or a closed port, always read as "the host did not answer" and stayed Fixed, not verified. A service check is now answered with a TCP connect (the Sentinel's `tcp` scheme, as the zombie check does), and a fix that closed the port is shown by the host answering on another port it was seen with. If every port is dead it is still not verified.
- **Sentinel result and enrollment calls are held tighter.** `POST /api/v1/sentinels/bootstrap`, the one call with no bearer token, answers 429 with `Retry-After` past 30 calls a minute from one address. A JSON body nested more than 32 levels (or deep enough to overflow the parser) is a clean 400 on every REST route, not a 500. A nuclei finding or template-test hit on another port than the port a task named is dropped (a bare host target still takes any port), and an IPv6 address in an nmap report matches the task's address however either is written.
- **A TLS audit or a UDP scan is not given up on while its Sentinel is still allowed to finish it.** The stage waited 600 seconds for a TLS audit and 900 for a UDP scan, and a Sentinel lets them run for 900 and 1500: a run that took longer was thrown away as late and its targets stayed due. The waits are now 1020 and 1560 seconds (`tests.test_sentinel_time_limits` holds every kind's wait above its tool's limit). New tests check each Sentinel result against what the real tools wrote in a lab (counts, nothing dropped, empty versus failed) and the check-out and check-in rules (one claimant, a result only from the Sentinel that holds the task, replay, lease, tenant).
- **The daily statistics counted a fixed finding once for every scan that kept a copy of it, and its mean days to fix could be negative.** A fixed finding is now one row of the current inventory, first seen when its earliest copy was made. Days already stored keep their old values; the next snapshot of each project is right.
- **The Targets table counted rows, not hosts.** A target with two names on one address and a certificate transparency row read 3 on Targets and 1 on Hosts. Both now count a host the way the Hosts page and the dashboard do, in one grouped query for the page.
- **The administrator alerts tell the truth.** On an install of two live remote Sentinels that completed 2,951 tasks in six hours, an alert said tasks were failing and that no Sentinel was online and the built-in Sentinel should be started (there was no built-in one), another counted 722 slot waits without saying how many slots or whether work was falling behind, and a fourth counted 121 failed lines that were a provider's key over its plan, a refused internal address, restart clean-up and an AI step put off. **Sentinel tasks are failing** now needs volume: a few stopped tasks beside thousands that completed, with Sentinels live, raise nothing, nor do tasks cut off by an update restart; the alert states failed, completed and live Sentinels. **Waiting for a free slot** states the slots, the completed count and whether the backlog is growing, and advises adding capacity only when work is falling behind. A discovery key that the provider says is over its plan or rate limited (any keyed source, not only SecurityTrails) is left out for three hours and noted once, no longer one failed line per discovery. A target the private-address guard refuses is one **skipped** line per host, shown on its scan, and is not queued again every round. Restart clean-up lines are **notice**, an AI step put off after the checks ran is **deferred**: neither counts as failing. The AI that writes the advice is given how many Sentinels are live and whether there is a built-in scanner, and its advice is dropped when it contradicts them; **AI-written checks cannot be tested yet** no longer tells a remote-Sentinel install to start a built-in Sentinel. Second pass: a kind that fails every time, or one dead Sentinel among several, is now an alert at any volume (each Sentinel and kind is judged on its own numbers and named), the restart grace is one-sided and per Sentinel, a validation with failed checks is never shown as deferred, no live Sentinel is a warning, flat saturation is said, the advice guard drops only the false sentence, a replaced key ends its pause, and the new activity statuses are drawn and documented.
- **The CVE feed tier filter (`/api/v1/cves?tier=`, `list_cve_feed`) read only the newest 2,000 CVEs, and its count was the size of that sample.** The database now filters by tier over the whole feed, so the count matches the dashboard's tier tiles.
- **Marking a finding fixed, accepted or false positive updates the dashboard numbers at once.** The person who closed it kept seeing it counted until the snapshot aged out.
- **A Sentinel with no IPv6 route is one notice, not a failed UDP scan per address.** On one production server the background UDP scan logged "Background UDP scan of <IPv6 address> failed (UdpScanError)" about fifty times in six hours, one per IPv6 address and none for IPv4: the Sentinel ran `nmap -6` correctly, had no route, and nmap reported 0 hosts scanned. Now the first one says "this Sentinel cannot reach IPv6 addresses", the address is recorded as not scanned for that reason (`udp-scan-noipv6`), no further IPv6 address is sent to a Sentinel for a day, and one address is tried again after that. An IPv4 address nmap could not reach is still a failed scan.
- **No IPv6 route is held against one Sentinel, and a WordPress site that did not answer is not always "gone".** The "cannot reach IPv6" flag was one key for the whole install, so a second Sentinel that has IPv6 never got IPv6 UDP work, and a single unroutable address switched IPv6 UDP off for everyone for a day. It is now kept per Sentinel (and per address), only that Sentinel is skipped, another live Sentinel of the same organization takes the address, and the address is recorded as not scanned only when every live Sentinel said it has no route; the whole Sentinel is skipped for a day only after two different public IPv6 addresses in a row came back with no route, and one scan that works lifts it. In the WordPress scan, when two or more sites were scanned and every one read as down, the stage stays failed and names the Sentinel (its own DNS or route out may be broken), and a site that the WordPress probe read within the last day and that now does not answer is a failure to retry, not a quiet skip. The states `unreachable`, `redirected` and `not_scannable` are on the host page in plain words and in the docs; a redirect or a site WPScan will not scan is no longer recorded as a finished scan that hides it for a week (a redirect is asked again after six hours, and a redirect to another target of the same project scans that name), and the one-week record is kept for sites that were scanned.
- **A WordPress scan that ends because the site is down, moved or cannot be scanned is not a failed scan, and a real tool failure says what failed.** WPScan's `scan_aborted` reasons were told apart only for "not WordPress", so a site that is down, one that redirects to another site, one hosted on wordpress.com, a run out of time and a database that could not be updated all read "the scan failed (see the host page)", and when it was the only site the host assessment failed with `Failed stages: wpscan`. They now end as `unreachable`, `redirected`, `not_scannable` (host states, listed under "not scanned" in the activity line) and never fail the stage; a crash, a time limit and a database update failure fail it and name the cause.
- **Housekeeping from a whole-project review:** a duplicate import, a duplicate item in a set, two `not in` tests written the long way, four unused locals, 134 unused imports, 19 functions that nothing called, and a docs line that named a script of the Sentinel repository as if it were this one.
- **The port-flood hygiene check reads only the checked domain's own addresses.** It counted the open ports of every address in the database for each domain it checked (the whole port table, 612,846 rows on the VPS, about 1 second per call, once per scan). It now counts only the domain's own. 19.3 seconds to 3.3 over 15 domains on the VPS, identical results.
- **The database refreshes its own table statistics.** After the kernel killed Postgres for memory on 11/9 the statistics were gone, and Postgres believed the port table held 1,313 rows when it held 612,846, so every query across it was planned for a tiny table. Autovacuum's analyze trigger is a percentage of the last known size, so it never corrected this. The 30 second resource tick now runs `ANALYZE` (17 seconds on the VPS, no blocking lock) when a table with rows has no statistics or none for 7 days (`cveFeed/db_stats.py`); one cheap read otherwise.
- **The Celery workers' garbage collector runs on a timer, not inside a greenlet switch.** On the VPS the workers crashed with SIGSEGV every 20 to 90 minutes. Every readable crash was in Python's cyclic garbage collector (a pointer chase through its object list) at a greenlet's first moments, with the thread's frame chain invalid. Python 3.14 runs an incremental collector that starts a step from any allocation, including one made while greenlet is switching stacks; Python's own crash handler then failed again in libgcc_s printing the C stack, which is why the kernel's line pointed at the unwinder. The automatic collector is now off in a gevent worker and a full collection runs every 30 seconds from a timer (`CVENGINE_GC_MANUAL=0` restores Python's own).
- **At most 2 requests to crt.sh are under way at once.** The certificate watcher spaced the starts of its lookups 1.5 to 2.5 seconds apart but never limited how many were in flight, so with replies taking up to 45 seconds a dozen overlapped, crt.sh answered 429 or timed out (127 and 85 in an hour on the VPS), and every miss became a retry, an exception record built inside a greenlet. The classic lane crashed in the garbage collector doing exactly that three times between 11/9 and 11/10. The count is shared by every worker through Redis and expires on its own, so a worker that dies mid-request cannot hold a place; a lookup that finds none free waits up to 30 seconds and then goes back to the queue.
- **Addresses that answer on every port keep 100 stored ports, not 65,535.** A Sentinel's full-port report is cut to the lowest 100 open ports of such an address (a load balancer, a tarpit or a filtering device), but lists stored before that cut stayed whole: on the VPS 38 addresses held 600,150 of the 613,262 stored ports. Every task that read a host pointing at one loaded all of those rows into Python: the work planner's census grew a worker by 1.3 GB per run, the Sentinel feed by 1.2 GB, and the lanes sat at the container's 4.5 GB ceiling within nine minutes of a restart, so the watchdog paused scanning and restarted celery in a loop. The update now trims those lists (`trim_flooded_ports`, a dry run unless `--apply`, run by itself once after the update); the port-flood finding still fires. After the trim on the VPS the whole celery container held 1.0 GB five minutes after a restart.
- **The server's own OpenVAS stack pins an `openvas-scanner` image that exists.** Greenbone removed the pinned digest from its registry on 11/10, so `make openvas-up` could not pull it. The pin moves to the current `stable` digest, the same one the Sentinel now pins.
- **A validation sweep no longer holds a worker slot for minutes.** A `validate_findings` batch (up to 200 findings of network checks, about 17 seconds each on the VPS) held one slot of the CVE-feed lane for minutes, and a handful of them held all four while the scheduler's own ticks (the work conductor, the Sentinel feed, the stage run ticks) waited behind them in the same queue: the conductor had not run for 35 minutes with the lane idle between batches. A batch now works for about 45 seconds, always at least one finding, and puts the rest at the back of the queue, so the ticks run in between. Nothing is dropped.
- **A stage run has one delayed drive pending, not a growing chain of them.** Every call to `drive` schedules the next one, so the minute tick and task-ended wake-ups each started another chain that kept itself going for ever: 9 waiting runs had 1,378 delayed drives queued on the VPS. Celery counts delayed messages against a worker's prefetch limit, so they held the CVE-feed lane's slots while it sat idle with 2,400 real tasks waiting. One guarded drive per run now (a wake-up when a scan is stopped is still sent at once).
- **A web archive that refuses us no longer holds the CVE-feed lane.** A failed Internet Archive lookup was never recorded, so every hygiene run retried every target and waited for each. After 3 failures in a row all lookups pause for 30 minutes, and a target whose lookup failed waits 6 hours (a success clears it; asking by hand still works).
- **The CVE-feed worker lane no longer stalls on the Sentinel feeder's "stalest hosts" query.** For a project of 12,700 host rows it took 129 seconds on the VPS (it re-scanned the project's assessments once per host, because there is no index on the assessed host), ran for every project every minute, and held the whole lane, so the lane stopped answering pings, the work conductor stopped, and its queue grew past 2,300. It is now four plain reads joined in code: 3.5 seconds for all 15 projects. The feeder also runs one at a time, so a slow run no longer stacks copies of itself behind it.
- **A secrets check marks a host as read only when the Sentinel got an answer from it.** A batch is up to 25 hosts, and the server recorded every one of them as read when the task finished, including a host the Sentinel skipped because its name did not resolve or its address is one a scanner never touches. Such a host now stays due (recorded as failed); the batch's other hosts are filed as before.
- **A database wait no longer freezes a whole gevent worker.** psycopg2 waits for Postgres in C, so while one task's query ran no other task on that worker ran, and it could not answer a ping. Gevent workers now install the usual wait callback (`reNgine/gevent_db.py`); a 3 second query left a 3.01 second stall in a ticker before and 0.10 after.
- **The watchdog does not restart a lane that is working but missed a ping.** A busy worker lane can miss a 3 second ping. On 11/9 the CVE-feed lane did, again and again, while finishing a correlation every few seconds, and the watchdog restarted celery three times, each time throwing away the lane's progress. A lane that finished a task in the last 90 seconds (each lane records it, `reNgine/lane_memory.py`) is busy, not gone.
- **The watchdog page no longer reads "not running" when a pass is merely slow.** On a loaded 2-core server with a worker lane silent, a pass took 18 seconds (each broadcast to a silent lane waits 3), and the 25-second limit cut the slower ones off before they recorded themselves, so the page said the watchdog was not ticking for 11 minutes. The limit is 40 seconds (the host's bounds 55 and 65) and a pass records that it started at once.
- **The watchdog no longer restarts a stack that has just finished an update.** Ten seconds after an update ended, a worker lane had not answered its first ping, and the watchdog restarted celery, twice, then reported "could not fix it" and stood down for an hour while the lanes were simply starting (the CVE-feed lane takes up to ten minutes). For ten minutes after an update finishes it stands aside, and its three minutes of silence count from the end of that window.
- **The watchdog does not restart a lane that is in the middle of one long task.** A lane running a single big job (a large port load, for example) finishes nothing for minutes and misses its 3 second ping, and the watchdog took that for a dead lane and recreated the container, which killed the job. Each worker records when a task starts and refreshes that record every 30 seconds while the task runs, with its process id; a lane whose worker is alive and in a task is called busy, for as long as the task runs (up to 12 hours), while a record nobody refreshes (a crashed worker) is ignored. The status says it is at work, not that it stopped.
- **The watchdog never returns an unacknowledged message while it cannot tell what the workers hold.** It put a message back on its queue when no worker answered or when one lane was silent, so a task a busy worker was still running could run a second time. Messages are now returned only when every lane answered the question, a skipped return is written to the Health event log, and messages that stay put for 30 minutes behind a silent lane raise a notice that says what is waiting and what to do. A message taken before a restart the host carried out, whose worker is certainly gone, is still returned.
- **The watchdog gives restarted workers time to start before it says it could not fix them.** After it restarted celery it checked two minutes later, and a lane that was still starting (the CVE-feed lane took about ten minutes on 11/9) was reported as "could not fix it" with an hour of silence. After a restart the host carried out it now waits for the same ten-minute warm-up an update gets, and says only that the workers have not answered yet; a lane that never returns is reported "could not fix it" ten minutes after the restart.
- **The watchdog does not call a lane hung because it is busy with one long task.** A lane with free slots whose queue did not shrink for ten minutes was restarted even while one task ran (and the notice said "a worker lane took no work"). A task running on a live worker (see the line above) now makes the lane busy, whatever its slot count.
- **A restart the host refuses for its daily limit is no longer reported as a failed fix.** The two restart rules each allowed 16 restarts a day while the host allows 24 in all, and when the host said no, the watchdog read that as "could not fix it" and went quiet for an hour. The restarts are now counted together, and a refusal for a limit is shown as held, with a warning that says the limit was reached and what to do.
- **AI calls to Anthropic use its prompt cache.** cvENGINE recorded cache tokens but never asked for the cache, so every call paid full price for its stage's fixed instructions: on the production install, about 12 of 28 million input tokens a week were the same system prompts sent again. Each stage's system prompt is now marked for the cache (the call's own data never is), on the server's own requests and on the AI jobs the reviewer container runs, with a lifetime per stage (1 hour for detection records, 5 minutes for bursts, off where a write would not be read). Only Anthropic's own host is sent the marker, a prompt under the model's minimum is sent as before, and `CVENGINE_AI_PROMPT_CACHE=off` turns it off. The usage API gains `cost_by_stage_day` (calls, tokens and cache-read share per day, stage and model, priced only at the operator's own rates), and the reviewer container's own two jobs now report their cache tokens. Ported from #604 onto 1.4.
- **The planner line no longer says "all Sentinel slots busy" when slots are free.** It said so whenever one kind's share came to zero, including with 6 of 10 slots idle. It now says which: the Sentinels able to run that kind have no free slot (and how many slots are free elsewhere), every slot is busy, or other kinds' waiting tasks already claim the free slots.
- **A search may negate at most three terms.** Every term, negated or not, is a pass over the project's hosts, and one search of fifteen negated words took a minute of database time. The fifteen-term limit stays for the rest.
- **Web requests have a database time limit.** The web server gives every statement 30 seconds (`CVENGINE_WEB_STATEMENT_TIMEOUT_MS`, 0 turns it off). Past it the REST API answers 503 and the MCP answers a tool error with a plain message, and the database stops the work instead of finishing it for a client that has gone. The migration, the workers and management commands have no limit. An MCP batch runs one `search_hosts` or `list_hosts` call.
- **A comment or tool argument with a NUL byte or a lone surrogate is no longer a 500.** Free text sent to the REST API and the MCP is cleaned once at the door. A JSON body over 256 KiB sent to the API is refused with 413 (the attack-string guard does not read bodies that large).
- **The Sentinels page shows the Sentinels at once.** The page polls the OpenVAS queue, and its explanation (why each host is or is not due) was worked out inside the request whenever its one-minute cache had lapsed: about 9 of the 10 seconds the request took for 15 projects on the production install, so the Sentinels appeared after 10 to 20 seconds. The last explanation is now kept for an hour and shown at once; once it is over a minute old a background refresh on the live-cache worker (which never scans) works it out again, one per project at a time. Only a project nobody has looked at yet is worked out in the request.
- **A template test cannot hang a worker on a catastrophic regular expression.** A matcher pattern the AI wrote is run with a 2 second limit in a child process and is reported as a matcher this check cannot evaluate.
- **The planner line on the Sentinels page shows only your own projects' work.** It used to include how many units the last round queued for every tenant.
- **A report for another tenant's or a missing scan is a 404.** It was a 500 (the same answer for both, so nothing leaked).
- **The AI reviewer no longer closes a finding the triage keeps a floor under.** A template finding whose matcher fired (or that a CVE template or a medium rating backs) and a CVE match whose advisory names no affected version could be closed as a false positive by one high-confidence model verdict. The close check now reads the triage's own hard-evidence floor (`triage.hard_evidence`, `match_policy.is_version_unknown`): such a finding stays open, with the reviewer's opinion recorded and the reason "hard evidence: ...". A CVE match whose version is inside the affected range is held the same way (the rule is the triage's, read from `triage.hard_evidence`, not copied): the AI never clears what triage says only a person or a published range may move. A verdict is now applied under a row lock on the finding, so it cannot overwrite a person's change made at the same moment.
- **A TLS test on a listener that completes no handshake no longer closes a proven finding.** testssl.sh prints "not vulnerable" for the vulnerability tests of a port it could not talk to. cvENGINE stored those as clear and closed earlier proven TLS matches (Heartbleed, ROBOT and the rest). A run with no handshake now clears nothing and leaves every earlier match and finding as it was.
- **Dependencies at their newest compatible releases.** Django 5.2.19 (the newest release of the 5.2 long-term support line), Markdown 3.11.3 and dnspython 2.9.2 (whatportis stays at 0.8: 0.8.2 pins a 2020 requests); the AI reviewer's Anthropic SDK 2.8.0 with what it pulls in (a fresh freeze); the reviewer image's Python base at its current digest; the Tailscale sidecar 1.104.6. Go stays at 1.27.3 here until the Sentinel library pin moves with it (the About page lists one Go for both images). The About page lists the new versions.
- **The Sentinels page never waits for the OpenVAS queue, even the first time.** With nothing kept (a new project, or the cache emptied by an update), the page used to work every project's queue out in the request: 55 seconds on the production install right after an update. It now asks the live-cache worker for the refresh and says which projects it is still working out; the queue appears at the next poll.
- **A host nmap gave up on is not recorded as fingerprinted.** When nmap marks a host timed out, the result is not stored as a host with no services and the host stays due for the next pass. Before, it counted as covered for the retention period.
- **A host a template pass never reached is not recorded as scanned.** nuclei exits cleanly with no output for a host it cannot connect to. The Sentinel now names the hosts it did not reach and the pass records them, and the names this server cannot resolve, as failed instead of done, so they are tried again and show as not scanned.
- **Full-port discovery can finish.** A task names 8 addresses (it was 64, which needs more than the Sentinel's 15 minute limit at the default rate). A scan that runs out of time reports the addresses it did not reach, and they stay due instead of reading as nothing open. The stage now waits at least as long as the Sentinel's own nmap and naabu limits, so a late result is no longer thrown away.
- **A DNS timeout on a mail name no longer resolves its email findings.** A name whose lookup failed on the Sentinel is unknown, and its open SPF and DMARC findings stay open.
- **OpenVAS: a host that never answered is not counted as scanned clean.** A Sentinel can list the hosts gvmd finished without a reply (offline or firewalled). Their earlier findings are no longer closed by that run.
- **bbot wildcard placeholders are not hosts.** The `_wildcard.<zone>` name bbot makes up for a wildcard zone is dropped at the door.
- **A nuclei scan no longer floods the SSH, mail or database port of the host it scans.** Every `host:port` of a task used to receive the whole template set (over 11,000 http templates and the raw tcp, javascript and ssl ones), so one weekly deep task made a host's sshd log 34,000 "banner exchange: invalid format" lines in 20 minutes and refuse its own administrator's logins (MaxStartups throttling). The server now labels each `host:port` of a nuclei task with what speaks there, from nmap's service name, the stored http result and the port number (`services`: `web`, `unknown`, or a protocol class such as `ssh`, `smtp`, `imap`, `tls`), splits a scan's ports into tasks of at most 6 non-web ports, and states the budget (`budget`: a pause between template runs, the seconds one port is kept busy, and a per-host request rate of 25 a second for web hosts). The Sentinel sends web ports the web templates only, every other port only its own protocol's templates one at a time with a pause between, only templates on the Sentinel library's pinned allowlist (default deny: nothing that sends credentials or changes state on the target), and a port with no class of its own or an unknown one the generic network templates, never an http template. Measured in a lab (throwaway listeners, nuclei 3.11.1): the old behaviour opened 6,226 connections to one port, 103 at once; now an ssh port gets 44 connections, 2 at once, at most 4 in a second, and OpenSSH 10.3 logs no penalty. A port the Sentinel could not fully check is listed in the activity feed with the reason and stays due, but is asked again at most once a day (ledger service `nuclei-failed`, 24 hours), not at every scan. Needs Sentinel library hevnsnt/cvENGINE-sentinel#79 (the pin in `web/requirements.txt` moves to it). [docs/SENTINELS.md](docs/SENTINELS.md)
- **Secret values do not outlive the step that files them.** The values a Sentinel reports for the secrets scan are scrubbed from the task row whether filing worked or raised, and a sweep scrubs any such result older than 10 minutes that was missed.
- **An update brings Redis and Postgres security and bug-fix releases.** `redis:alpine` and `postgres:18-alpine` were pulled once, at install, so an install kept those versions for ever (the production VPS ran Redis 8.10.2 with 8.10.3 out). The update now pulls both before it stops anything, as it does the proxy, and recreates each only when its image moved. Postgres stays on 18: a new major version is a data migration and never comes from a pull. A failed pull keeps the images on the host.
- **Nuclei findings from a Sentinel keep their CVSS score, references and remediation text** (with the CVE ids, request and response the server already accepted), and the result names the template release the run used.
- **The watchdog no longer runs out of restarts by mid-morning.** R1 (workers answer) and R2 (workers take their work) were limited to 4 actions a day, and the host to 8 restarts a day. On a worker container that crashes every quarter of an hour the 4 were spent by 9:28 am, and the watchdog then left a silent lane hung for 50 minutes ("Reached the limit of 4 actions a day"). R1 and R2 may now act 16 times a day and the host 24; the loop protection is unchanged: the 15 minute gap, 2 an hour, and the hold of an hour after an action that did not fix it.
- **The dashboard asks the database for the host list once, not seven times, and keeps a slow count for longer.** Counting hosts (one row per name, names on the same addresses folded) is the dearest query there is. The headline counts collapsed the inventory twice, the graph twice, the Hosts search four times and the daily snapshot twice, and every open Dashboard recomputed the headline counts, the ticker counts and the graph every 5 seconds, so on a busy install one collapse was always running (up to 9.9 s each, 2 cores at a load of 13). Each now collapses once and the three live snapshots of one scope share it. A headline snapshot that takes long to compute is now kept for ten times what it took (at most two minutes, never less than the ten seconds it had), so the refresher stops recomputing it every 5 seconds. Every write that moves one of those numbers now drops the kept copy at once, after it is saved: closing or reopening a finding or CVE match (one by one, in bulk, from the page, the API or an agent's fix), a scan that finishes, and deleting, archiving, restoring or moving a host or a target or deleting a project. The list of live hosts behind the ticker is kept for 5 seconds at most, not for the longer time. Every number is the same as before.
- **The API token list column has a database default.** `dashboard_apitoken.suffix` was added NOT NULL with only a Python default, so the update rehearsal failed on the Batch 5 head ("the update added a column the old code cannot insert around") and a token created by the previous version during an update or rollback would have failed to insert. Migration 0035 sets the database default.
- **Hygiene, Sentinels and Overview show your data on the first screen.** At 1366x768 no page of the three showed a data row (Hygiene's first issue was 810 px down, Sentinels' first card 749 px, Overview's first list 1480 px).
