Read these before updating an existing install. The updater makes a database backup before it migrates (`scripts/db-backup.sh`), and that backup is the way back from the migrations that drop data.

- **Sentinels.** A Sentinel's token now has its own scope (`sentinel`) and lasts 90 days; a heartbeat renews the same token in place when under 60 days are left. A Sentinel that was off for more than 90 days must be enrolled again. `network:internal` is now granted by the server (**Settings of a Sentinel, Where it runs**), not by what a Sentinel declares: existing Sentinels that declared it are carried over by the migration. A Sentinel client older than this release keeps working but cannot enforce the template policy; update it from the Sentinels page.
- **Stored sign-ins and certificates.** A stored sign-in (authenticated checks) is sent only over a verified certificate. A target whose certificate cannot be verified needs the per-target **Trust this certificate anyway** switch. The generated web certificate now lasts 397 days and is renewed before it expires (`scripts/renew-certs.sh`; `make certs`, or `CERT_FORCE=1`, makes a new one at once).
- **Removed.** The legacy `/api/*` routes the interface no longer uses, the bare-domain fallback of the protected-media route, and the legacy models nothing wrote to (the `scanEngine` and `startScan` legacy tables, `recon_note.TodoNote`, `targetApp.Organization`, and the raw column of `CVEEntry`). Their data goes with the migrations.
- **Proxy.** **Settings, Proxy** now applies to the scans that run on this server (web probes, nuclei, bbot, WPScan, testssl.sh, wafw00f); nmap, naabu and the UDP sweeps stay direct. Private, link-local and tailnet addresses skip the proxy (`CVENGINE_PROXY_PRIVATE=1` changes that). The stored proxy list is encrypted by a migration.
- **Unknown versions.** A CVE that publishes no affected version range, even an actively exploited one, now makes a match marked **Needs validation** with the lowest heat and a tiny, capped share of the host score. It is never announced as a new Fire match and the model cannot raise or dismiss it. It is judged on its version as soon as the feed publishes a range.
- **Defaults that changed.** A name that resolves only into a private network is archived after 3 days like any other unscannable name (`CVENGINE_TTL_UNRESOLVED_HOST`, was 5 days) and is looked at again daily. A host that crashes OpenVAS three times in a row is set aside. Project API keys can be set and deleted by project administrators only. A single paste of exclusions is capped at 100 lines. The per-project counts in the system status are reused for 60 seconds (`CVENGINE_STATUS_CACHE_SECONDS`).
- **Bug bounty.** The automated-testing gate now works per host (the scope index) and covers the full port scan, the weekly deep Nuclei pass, WPScan's thorough mode and OpenVAS.

### Security and review fixes

This release is the result of a review of the whole repository. The main changes: Sentinel token scope, result binding to the dispatched addresses and a 24 MB result route; the approved-template snapshot and `-etags dos` for Nuclei on a Sentinel, plus the template bundle endpoints (`GET /api/v1/sentinel/template-policy` and `/bundle`); the heartbeat `running` list that frees a task a Sentinel no longer runs; OpenVAS runs that report no scanned host are inconclusive and retried up to three times; input guard, SSRF and slug fixes; the Redis false critical; the nmap XML size cap; migration `startScan` 0020 for an address shared by two organizations; efficiency indexes and counts on the hottest pages; updater safety (backup, retries, signature checks); container hardening; and frontend fixes (the phone Back crash, the API keys panel, daylight-saving chart axes).


### Tool updates

Every pinned tool is at its newest release: wpscan 4.2.0, naabu 2.7.0, testssl.sh 3.2.4, python-gvm 27.10.1, tzdata 2026.6 (IANA 2026f) and the Tailscale sidecar image 1.104.4. wpscan 4.2.0 caps how much of one response it reads (`--max-response-size`, 16 MiB by default), so a hostile target cannot exhaust a scanner's memory with an endless reply. naabu 2.7.0 scans likely ports first on a full-port run. testssl.sh 3.2.4 HTML-escapes server-controlled text in its HTML report (cvENGINE reads only the JSON). Tailscale 1.104.4 fixes the issues in bulletins TS-2026-014 to -017 (SSH Unix socket forwarding as root, SSH session memory growth, two in Tailscale Serve); the tailnet check now flags SSH-enabled devices on an older client for the two SSH bulletins.

### Added

- **The About page lists what cvENGINE runs on today.** It was still the 1.0 list: it now credits testssl.sh, WPScan, wafw00f, aardwolf, node2shell-scanner, the optional Greenbone (OpenVAS) and Tailscale add-ons, the Sentinel client, VulnCheck KEV, the Internet Archive, runZero, the bug bounty scope and rating data, the AI back ends and the PDF typeface. A test keeps it in step with the scanner list in `THIRD-PARTY.md`.
- **Move a host to another organization or project.** On a host's **Manage** tab, **Org / Project** shows where the host lives and a picker (grouped by organization, limited to the projects you can write to) hands it to another project (REST `POST projects/{slug}/hosts/{host}/move`, MCP `move_host`; write access to both projects). Everything that could refuse the move is checked first: an exclusion in the destination, a target of that name elsewhere in the organization, or a host that is a target holding other hosts. Then the host's data leaves the old project, a record of the move keeps that project's discovery and scans from finding the host again, and the host is added to the destination as a target and scanned there. Moving it back lifts the block. `GET /api/v1/projects` now names each project's organization (`org`, `org_name`).
- **The Live page says where things are.** Attack surface tiles show `Organization / Project` under the host name (the organization is left out when every tile shares it), the oldest-issue tile names the issue and its host (`/cve/api/stats` adds `oldest_open_what` with `severity`, `title` and `host`), the Sentinels Provision form picks the organization from a list of the ones you manage instead of a typed slug, and Settings groups its tabs in a column beside the panel (AI and access, Scanning, Alerts and reports, System) so no tab scrolls off screen.
- **A second tour GIF** for 1.3 (Sentinels and internal scanning, Settings and Live) in the README, with the seed data, recording script and GIF builder under `scripts/demo/` so it can be remade.
- **A WPScan failure now says why.** WPScan prints "Scan Aborted: --url ..." (a refused option or address, exit code 1) on its standard output, which the worker threw away, so the host page and the activity feed only said "exit 1". The worker now keeps that line (clipped, with the API token removed), and a failure with no output at all says what the exit code means. A site behind an edge that challenges the scanner is still reported as blocked (WPScan exit code 4), not as a failure. A Sentinel's report carries the line in its `stderr_tail`, so it needs a client that sends WPScan's standard output there.
- **An update no longer shows its build window as errors.** The new files are under the running containers while the update builds, so a web worker that restarted then loaded the new code against the old database and every call that needed a new column failed with a 500 (Sentinel polls and heartbeats among them) until the migration ran. Those calls now get a 503 with `Retry-After` while migrations are really waiting (`reNgine/schema_guard.py`), which Sentinels and agents treat as "try again", the proxy shows its maintenance page for, and the log notes once a minute. A missing column once everything is migrated is still a 500.
- **Onboarding email: a new person gets their username and a link to choose their own password.** An App Admin sets an SMTP server under **Settings, Email** (server, port, STARTTLS or SSL/TLS or none, optional login, From address and name, a switch to verify the server certificate, an optional link address, and a **Send test message** button; the password is encrypted and write-only, a login is refused over an unencrypted connection, and a link-local or cloud metadata server address is refused). When someone is added under **Administration, People** with an email address and no typed password, they receive a designed message (HTML with a plain-text part, dark mode aware, no remote images) with their username and a button to **Set your password**; no password is created or shown. Saving the password signs them in (except someone with two-step sign-in, who is sent to the sign-in page so the code is still asked). The link is signed, works once (it is bound to the account's credentials, so setting a password or an administrator's reset ends it), expires after 7 days (`CVENGINE_INVITE_DAYS`, 1 to 30), changes nothing when only opened (mail scanners follow links), and is slowed down against guessing. It is built from the address set under Settings, Email or else `https://` and `DOMAIN_NAME`, never from a request header. **Reset password** now emails a link to a reset page the same way (the old password stops working at once, sessions and API tokens end). If mail is off, the person has no address, or the send fails, you are shown a temporary password exactly as before (with the reason when a send failed). **Manage, Send the link again** issues a fresh link and ends the earlier ones for a person who has not chosen a password yet. Sending and setting are audited (`user.invite_sent`, `user.invite_failed`, `user.reset_password`, `account.invite_accepted`, `email.update`, `email.test`). See [Tenancy](docs/TENANCY.md#onboarding-email).
- **Sentinels: the weekly deep Nuclei pass now dispatches too, and every hand-off and check-in shows in the AI activity feed.** `weekly_deep_nuclei` offers its nuclei stream to a live, eligible Sentinel first via the same `agentapi.sentinel_dispatch.wrap_nuclei_stream` seam the per-scan light and targeted passes already use (a new `nuclei_deep` `SentinelTask` kind, same result shape, no new client-side code needed). Separately, every dispatch attempt (nmap, naabu, any nuclei pass) now logs one activity line while it waits and settles it once the wait ends, naming the Sentinel and the outcome on success or a reported failure, or saying plainly that no Sentinel checked in and the stage ran locally, visible on both the Live page and the Activity page with no changes to either, since both already render this activity kind. See [Architecture](docs/ARCHITECTURE.md#sentinels). No new migration.

- **A CVE match opens on three cards instead of a wall of text.** What was found (the product and version, how it was seen, the advisory range), Confidence (the rules' match score as a meter, with a check mark, cross or dash for the version, template, distribution, live check and hard evidence), and the AI assessment (the verdict, what the model said when the rules overrode it and why, its reasoning, the severity view and why it matters, long text cut to a few lines with Read more). Below them, what to do (the fix, the status and Validate now), then folded sections with a one-line summary each: Confirm it yourself, About the CVE, Seen and history, AI details, and the validation job. Threat signals (exploited in the wild, public exploit, EPSS) sit on one line. The same layout opens scanner findings.
- **Full first-pass coverage now outranks deep scanning, instance-wide.** The background full port scan and the UDP scan both check, before picking a target, whether any project (not bug-bounty-blocked) anywhere on this instance still has a host missing its core first pass (nmap, the light nuclei pass, CVE correlation, the targeted nuclei pass); if so the whole firing is skipped and logged ("Full-port scan deferred: N hosts across M projects still need a first pass") instead of spending a 65,535-port sweep or a UDP exposure check on one address while basic coverage is incomplete elsewhere. The check is a single bounded, cheap existence query per project (no full count, short-circuits on the first due host), reusing `cveFeed.assessment_review.host_coverage_due` rather than a second query. A backlog that is only bounty-blocked, or only there because scanning is paused instance-wide, does not gate deep scanning forever. See [Pipeline](docs/PIPELINE.md#keeping-scans-fast-and-polite).
- **Hosts that never get a scannable IP are now archived on their own, shorter timeline, and automatically restored if they come back.** The existing 30-day inactive-archive review only ever covered a host that vanished from enumeration (`HostState.retired_at`); a host still enumerated every run but that has never once resolved to a scannable IP kept its host assessments ending inconclusive forever. A new streak on `HostState` (first-failure timestamp, last-failure timestamp, a count), updated from each finished host assessment, resets on a single success so a host that resolves fine and is merely unassessed is never affected. A new daily, checkpointed review (`cvengine-daily-unresolved-archive-review`, same shape as the 30-day one) archives a host once that streak has lasted `CVENGINE_TTL_UNRESOLVED_HOST` (hours, default 5 days) with no success, skipping a pinned, excluded, or currently queued or running host. Once archived this way it is picked up by the same daily rediscovery sweep every other archived host already gets (it keys off `archived_at` alone), so it is rechecked once a day and restored and reassessed automatically. See [Pipeline](docs/PIPELINE.md#changes) and [Configuration](docs/CONFIGURATION.md). Needs migration 0105.
- **Sentinels: the foundation for scanners you run on your own network.** A Sentinel is a scan-stage agent (`Agent` `kind='scanner'`): it registers, pulls nmap, naabu and nuclei work off a project's queue (`SentinelTask`, a new model alongside `AgentTask`), runs it and reports typed, clipped results back, with the same identity, scope, admin approval, suspend/revoke, heartbeat and rate limiting every agent already has. Before a scan stage runs its tool, it offers the work to a live, eligible, in-scope Sentinel with free capacity first; a project with none registered pays one cheap existence check and runs exactly as it always has (the critical regression property this phase's tests prove), and a Sentinel's result is applied through the exact same persistence code local execution already uses. An org admin or App Admin generates a one-time, 30-minute enrollment secret per project (**Sentinels** page, or `GET/POST projects/{slug}/sentinels/enroll`), which a Sentinel trades for its own token on its first call (`POST sentinels/bootstrap`, no prior credential needed). This phase is the server-side contract, queueing, dispatch point and admin UI only, for nmap, naabu and the two nuclei passes; bbot, testssl, the bucket/vhost/UDP sweeps and WPScan are untouched and keep running locally, and the actual downloadable Sentinel client (a Docker image) is a follow-up built against this contract. See [Architecture](docs/ARCHITECTURE.md#sentinels), [the API](docs/AGENT-API.md#sentinels-scan-stage-agents) and [Front end](docs/FRONTEND.md#sentinels). New setting: `CVENGINE_SENTINEL_DISPATCH_TIMEOUT`. Needs migration 0002 (agentapi).
- **Sentinels: the actual downloadable client, in its own repository, and one-blob provisioning.** The Sentinel client ships at [hevnsnt/cvengine-sentinel](https://github.com/hevnsnt/cvengine-sentinel), a small, standalone Docker Compose service kept out of this codebase so provisioning one never needs a clone of cvENGINE itself: `docker compose up -d` with a provisioning blob in `CVENGINE_SENTINEL_BLOB` is the whole install. The Sentinels page's **Provision** button (also `?format=blob` on `sentinels/enroll`) base64-encodes the existing enrollment payload into that one copy-pasteable blob (`agentapi.sentinel_broker.encode_enrollment_blob`; still a plain bearer token over HTTPS, no new crypto or key material); the client decodes it once, bootstraps, and persists its token (0600, in a named volume) so a restart never needs the blob again. The `generate_sentinel_enrollment` MCP tool returns the blob plus a `setup` block of four ready-to-run shell commands (clone, configure, run, verify), and the Sentinel repository's own `AGENTS.md` walks an AI agent with Docker and shell access to a target machine through the whole provisioning flow unattended. The client long-polls for work, runs nmap, naabu or a nuclei pass as a fixed argv list against only the task's own server-issued target (never a shell command, never free text from the server), parses the tool's real output into the typed result shapes the broker expects, heartbeats every 60 seconds, and on SIGTERM finishes or releases in-flight tasks rather than abandoning them. A nuclei finding a Sentinel reports now also carries `matcher_name` end to end (`_clean_findings`, `sentinel_dispatch._finding_to_nuclei_line`), so a Sentinel-run tech-detect template hit creates a `Technology` row the same way a local run already does. The Sentinel image's own Dockerfile pins nmap, naabu and nuclei to the same versions `web/Dockerfile` pins here, kept in sync by hand across the two repositories. `get_system_status`'s `system.sentinels` now reports registration counts, how many are live, scan-stage queue depth, the last day's completed/failed/expired `SentinelTask`s and a sample of recent errors, with a high failure share flagged as a problem, so Sentinel health is visible in one call. See [Architecture](docs/ARCHITECTURE.md#sentinels), [the API](docs/AGENT-API.md#sentinels-scan-stage-agents) and [Front end](docs/FRONTEND.md#sentinels). No new migration.
- **Sentinels: an enrollment can now cover an organization or the whole instance, not just one project.** `orgs/{slug}/sentinels/enroll` (an admin of that organization, or an App Admin) scopes a Sentinel to every project of that organization, present and future, by reusing the existing `scope.orgs` resolution (`agentapi.agent_scope.project_slugs_in_scope` already resolved it dynamically at claim time, so a project the organization gains later needs no new enrollment). `sentinels/enroll` with no project or organization named (App Admin only) scopes a Sentinel to the whole server, present and future, via a new `scope.instance` flag. The Sentinels page's scope selector (Project, Organization, Instance) and the `generate_sentinel_enrollment` MCP tool's optional `project`/`org` arguments (at most one; neither means instance-wide) cover all three the same way, each still returning the one-time, 30-minute provisioning blob. See [Architecture](docs/ARCHITECTURE.md#sentinels), [the API](docs/AGENT-API.md#sentinels-scan-stage-agents) and [Front end](docs/FRONTEND.md#sentinels). No new migration.
- **A friendly page while an update is applied.** From the end of the build until the new web service answers, and whenever the application does not answer at all (502, 503, 504), the proxy shows a self-contained animated page (two engineers over a blueprint) that says cvENGINE is being rebuilt, and reloads by itself when the site is back, instead of a bare "Server Error (500)". `scripts/update-deploy.sh` sets and always removes the flag file `config/nginx/state/maintenance`; `make up` clears a stale one. An administrator who has the page open gets the same scene over the whole page from the moment the host picks the update up (the first update included, as it is a static file), with the current step as its status line, and is taken back to `/cve/` when the update is done. Other visitors get it from the proxy from the update after the one that delivers it.
- **Third-party suppliers: a host card per supplier domain, and a search of every vendor by technology.** A project can list the critical vendors it depends on and cannot fix for itself (name, tier critical, high or normal, the exact domain names, a contact). Each name gets a Shodan-style host card: the response headers and `Server` and `X-Powered-By` banners, the TLS certificate (issuer, names, validity, protocol, cipher) and the technology stack with versions where the response states them (web server, language, framework, CMS, JavaScript libraries, read with the fingerprinting owned hosts already use), with the time it was last checked. The **Suppliers** page, `GET projects/{slug}/suppliers/hosts?tech=php,node` and the `search_supplier_technology` tool search all of a project's suppliers by technology, so you can ask the vendors that run PHP or Node.js whether they are patched; **Copy message** (and `get_supplier_message`) builds a plain-text note to send them, and nothing is sent by cvENGINE. A supplier is not an owned host: it never becomes a target, host or scan input, and discovery, bbot, nmap, nuclei, subdomain enumeration, host assessments, remediation assignment, SLA clocks, hygiene lists and stats never see it. Only an organization admin or an App Admin adds or removes one (members and auditors read); a name that is one of the project's own targets, an address, a wildcard, a URL, a private name or an excluded name is refused. The checks are passive and polite (a TLS handshake and normal page requests on ports 443 and 80, DNS records, one GET per path of a fixed list of 11 well-known admin and login pages, never a guessed path): public addresses only through the pinned transport, stopped by a scan pause, skipped for a bug bounty project that has not confirmed automated testing, a shared global request gap, one Redis-claimed sweep a day on its own `supplier_queue`, each supplier once a week, and caps on suppliers per project, names per supplier and requests per sweep. Secondary and informational, kept in their own table so no owned-host count moves: posture findings for the certificate (expiry, name, chain, weak key or signature, TLS 1.0 and 1.1), email authentication (SPF, DKIM, DMARC, MTA-STS), missing HSTS and security headers, an admin or login page that answers, and the known CVEs of an announced version (same matching rules as owned hosts but strict: a bounded affected range only, nothing behind an edge, a distribution build one step lower, never validated). A finding resolves by itself when a later check no longer finds it, can be accepted by an admin, and says what to ask the supplier. See [Pipeline](docs/PIPELINE.md#third-party-suppliers), [Configuration](docs/CONFIGURATION.md) and the [API](docs/AGENT-API.md). New settings: `CVENGINE_SUPPLIERS`, `CVENGINE_SUPPLIERS_MAX_PER_PROJECT`, `CVENGINE_SUPPLIERS_MAX_DOMAINS`, `CVENGINE_SUPPLIERS_MAX_REQUESTS`, `CVENGINE_TTL_SUPPLIER`, `CVENGINE_TTL_SUPPLIER_FAILED`. Needs migration 0102 (and 0103, the daily schedule).
- **Check coverage on every host.** With this many checks there was no way to tell what had run on a host and what had not. A host record now carries `check_coverage` (REST `hosts` and `search`, MCP `list_hosts` and `search_hosts`): `{done, applicable, paused, checks: [{id, label, state, last_run, ttl_hours, detail}]}`, and the host page's Overview says "NN of NNN applicable checks done" and names the first few that are due. Only checks that apply to the host are counted: no TLS port, no TLS audit; no sign of WordPress, no WPScan; UDP only for addresses it would scan; a switched-off check, a project the bug bounty gate keeps out of the slow sweeps, and an excluded or archived host count nothing. A check is `done` inside the ledger window of its kind (`CVENGINE_TTL_<KIND>` overrides apply), `due` when it never ran, is stale or failed last time, and `not_applicable` with the reason. One registry (`cveFeed/check_coverage.py`) lists each check once, with its applicability rule and where its last run is recorded; it reads existing rows (the service ledger, route and certificate rows, scan stages), needs no migration, and a page of hosts costs a fixed number of batched queries, limited to the page's own projects. A test fails when a ledger kind, a sweep or a scan stage exists that is neither in the registry nor listed as deliberately excluded, so a new check cannot skip it. `nuclei-light`, `nuclei-cve`, `rdp-shot` and `node2shell` are now listed in the ledger's window table (24 hours, as before). See [Pipeline](docs/PIPELINE.md#check-coverage).
- **A How cvENGINE works page.** The Live page now links to a short, high-level description of the pipeline (discover, map, match, prove, prioritize), with the tools behind each step. A test fails until the page has been reviewed for the version being released, so it stays correct.
- **A TLS audit with testssl.sh.** A slow daily sweep (`testssl_sweep`, its own `testssl_queue`, one port at a time, longest-waiting first, 40 per run, stopped by a scan pause) tests the protocols, cipher categories, forward secrecy, renegotiation and the named TLS vulnerabilities (Heartbleed, CCS injection, ROBOT, POODLE, FREAK, DROWN, Logjam, SWEET32, Ticketbleed, RC4, CRIME, Winshock and more) of every TLS port nmap found open, with STARTTLS for mail and directory ports, at the address the platform vetted. Findings are `tlsa_*` hygiene findings; a vulnerability testssl.sh proves is also hard CVE evidence (`testssl` matches) that is closed when a later test finds it gone. testssl.sh 3.2.3 is pinned by checksum in the image (with `bind9-dnsutils`), listed in the SBOM and in the release gate, and its version is in `get_system_status`.
- **The upgrade rehearsal tests what went wrong in production.** It now starts the update the way cron does (the watcher, with a bare environment), seeds 5000 CVE matches (half under an older wording a migration rewrites), compares the schema before and after, runs a functional smoke test inside the updated install (MCP and its token lookup, tools reading the changed tables, the main pages), applies a second update with the scripts the first one installed, and approves it again to check nothing is rebuilt. The updater's own tests kill it at each step (build, migrate, before the restart, during it) and check that the next run finishes the job.
- **How long the worst open item has sat.** `/cve/api/stats` now returns `oldest_open_days` (whole days), `oldest_open_kind` (`cve`, `scanner` or `hygiene`) and `oldest_open_id` (the row to link to): the longest-open critical or high item among the exposed CVE matches (CVSS 7.0 or more, counted from the first scan that held the match), the actionable scanner findings and the open hygiene findings, with the same project and organization scoping as the other stats. Fixed, accepted-risk, ruled-out and resolved items do not count, and all three are `null` when nothing qualifies. Defined once in `cveFeed.counts.oldest_open`.

- **Other applications on a known host: other sites on the same address, and applications under subdirectories.** Two slow, polite background sweeps (own `vhost_queue`, once a week per question, global pause, exclusions, the scannable gate, a global and a per-host request gap, `CVENGINE_VHOST=0` and `CVENGINE_SUBDIR_APPS=0` to turn them off) look only for applications that could carry a known CVE, and are not content discovery: GET only, no body, no cookie, no credential, no redirects followed, no wordlist. The virtual host sweep asks each web address once for each name the project already knows (its hosts, the names in the certificates seen on that address, certificate transparency names) that is in scope and not yet recorded there, with that Host header and server name, compares the answer with the address's default response and with the name's own site, and adds a distinct application as an alias or host of the project (names that resolve to the same addresses stay one host); an out-of-scope name is never asked or added. The subdirectory sweep asks each web host for about 40 well-known application paths (`web/cveFeed/data/subdir_apps.json`, with the product each indicates) and for at most 10 directories its own crawled and archived URLs, `robots.txt` and `sitemap.xml` show, 60 requests at most per host, ignores a server that answers every path alike, and reads the product and version from the response: a path that only answered is a lead, a product that states a version becomes a Technology and reaches CVE matching (product and version, as always), and a product with no version is recorded as such. An edge that challenges is recorded and never retried. Results are `vhost` and `subapp` probe observations, shown on the host page ("Also served here", "Applications under subdirectories") and counted in `get_system_status`. See [Pipeline](docs/PIPELINE.md#other-sites-and-applications-on-a-host).
- **Authenticated checks.** An App Admin or the organization's admin can store request headers and a cookie for a target they may test (the **Sign-in** button on the Targets page or the REST API), so Nuclei and the web route check see the signed-in application and not only its login page. The values are encrypted at rest, write-only (only the header names are ever shown) and never logged; a credential is sent only to the exact host and `scheme:port` origins it was stored for, never on a redirect, and not after its expiry. Nuclei reads them from a private 0600 secrets file that is deleted afterwards, never from the command line; every stored request, response and error has them redacted, and a finding seen while signed in is tagged `seen-while-authenticated`. Every credential has a mandatory expiry (30, 90 or 120 days, or an explicit never; omitting it is a 400) and an explicit scope: one named host, or all hosts of the target chosen deliberately with a warning (a blank host alone is refused, never the whole target); the list shows the days left. The MCP server can list credentials (names, scope, expiry, no values) and revoke them but has no tool that sets one, so a secret never passes through a model's context. Use a dedicated low-privilege test account. See [Pipeline](docs/PIPELINE.md#authenticated-checks). Needs migration 0097.
- **OpenVAS sets itself up: choose what to scan and nothing else.** Settings > OpenVAS was a form for a GMP user name, a password, a switch and a typed list of hosts, plus two commands on the host. It is now one page, **Administration > OpenVAS** (also the Settings tab), where an App Admin or an Org Admin ticks whole organizations or single projects, as many as they like, limited to what they administer. An App Admin switches the add-on on, which makes the choices available to Org Admins; the host agent starts the Greenbone containers (`scripts/openvas-sync.sh`, with a free-memory check), the worker replaces the default login with a generated one and saves it encrypted, and scans begin when the feed has loaded; the page says which of those it is waiting on and offers Try again. An organization choice covers its later projects too. A project with no host list now scans its own hosts (the same vetting as before: exclusions, archived hosts, prohibited and tailnet addresses; stalest first, a few at a time, once a week), and a typed list still limits it through the API. Only an App Admin switches it on or off for everyone; a missing host agent is shown with its install command; the choices are audited. New model `OpenvasOrg` (migration 0106).
- **OpenVAS (Greenbone) as an optional scanner, off by default.** An App Admin can add Greenbone's community scanner beside nuclei and nmap. It costs nothing until it is turned on: its containers are the compose profile `openvas` (`COMPOSE_PROFILES=openvas`, `make openvas-up`; the program images pinned by tag and digest, the feed data images floating on purpose so the vulnerability definitions keep updating, a minimal set that exposes gvmd's GMP socket; only the scanner container gets `NET_RAW` and `NET_ADMIN`, nothing runs privileged), its beat entry is created disabled and the **Settings > OpenVAS** switch enables it, and no queue consumer or task does anything while it is off. `make openvas-user` replaces Greenbone's default `admin` login with a generated password set over GMP (never on a command line) and stores it encrypted. A project is scanned only after it opts in and lists hosts, each of which must already be one of its hosts; excluded or archived hosts, names that resolve somewhere prohibited and every tailnet address are refused, and GVM is given only the vetted numeric address. The sweep is slow (a few hosts per task, one task at a time, each host weekly, a throttled "cvENGINE safe scan" copy of Full and fast without the Denial of Service and Brute force attacks families and with `safe_checks`, verified before every use) on its own `openvas_queue`, and it refuses to start while the feed is not loaded (Settings says why and how far it is), while the platform's resource guard says memory is short, or while scanning is paused. Every result that names a CVE becomes a Vulnerability (source `openvas`) and CVE evidence, deduplicated by host, port and NVT OID; only a quality of detection of 80 or more is hard evidence (`template_result` `vulnerable`), a lower one never confirms anything, a result a later complete scan no longer reports is resolved, and the NVT's text is stored as clipped plain text. Needs `python-gvm` 27.10.0 (installed by the update's image rebuild) and migrations 0099 and 0100. See [docs/OPENVAS.md](docs/OPENVAS.md), which also lists what could not be verified against a real GVM.
- **A software bill of materials with every release.** Each release now carries `cvengine-<tag>.sbom.cdx.json` (CycloneDX 1.5), listing the pinned scanner tools with the checksums of what is downloaded, the Python packages and the service images, and what it cannot vouch for (nuclei's templates, floating image tags) is marked. `python3 scripts/generate_sbom.py` produces it for any checkout, and every pull request's build keeps it as an artifact.
- **Every scanner tool is pinned, and a release checks them all.** nuclei, naabu and bbot (and its `tzdata`) were installed as "latest" at build time, so a rebuild could change what a scan does. They are now pinned to their current releases like nmap, WPScan and wafw00f. `scripts/check_tool_versions.py` compares every pin (also the Go toolchain and the Tailscale image) with the newest release, the weekly check opens an issue for the ones behind, and a release is held until none is: the pull request into `master` fails the new *Tools up to date for release* check and the Release workflow stops, unless the newer version was looked at and the pull request is labelled `tools-reviewed` (or `tools_reviewed` is ticked on a manual run).
- **Public and dangling cloud storage is found.** A slow daily sweep (its own queue, `bucketscan_queue`) looks for AWS S3, Google Cloud Storage and Azure Blob buckets that belong to a project's domains: buckets a host CNAMEs to or is served from, storage URLs in crawled, archived and stored data, and a short list of name guesses built from the domain label (`example-backup`, `example-assets`, `example-dev` and so on). It asks only the storage platforms' documented endpoints, with anonymous HTTPS GET and HEAD, one listing page and one HEAD of a listed object, never an object's content, at a global rate of a few requests a second. A public listing of a bucket the project's own hosts point at is high (critical when the names look like backups, database dumps or keys), a public listing of a name guess is medium and says "ownership unconfirmed, verify the bucket is yours", a CNAME or reference to a bucket name nobody has registered is a high takeover finding, and a bucket that exists and refuses listing is only an observation. Findings are ordinary hygiene findings (`bkt_listing`, `bkt_takeover`) with the usual statuses, triage and API; at most 20 object names are kept and names that look like secrets are redacted. See [docs/PIPELINE.md](docs/PIPELINE.md#cloud-storage-exposure). Settings: `CVENGINE_BUCKETS`, `CVENGINE_BUCKETS_GUESS`, `CVENGINE_BUCKETS_RPS`, `CVENGINE_BUCKETS_MAX_PER_SWEEP`, `CVENGINE_TTL_BUCKETS`. Needs migration 0095.
- **One-click PDF reports.** **Download PDF report** on the Live page (once a project is selected) and on a host's Manage tab builds a PDF of the project or the host: a cover with the scope, an executive summary (fire, smoke and ice, severity, top risks), a host table, one section per finding with its evidence, the exact command or probe that showed it, remediation text and the status, assignee and notes people recorded, TLS and email hygiene, and a method and validity page. Open findings only by default, with a switch for fixed and accepted-risk ones. It is built by a worker task (`generate_report`), written with a random name under `/usr/src/scan_results/.reports/` and downloadable only by the person who asked for 24 hours; the weekly maintenance removes stragglers. Any role that can read the project can export it, and the role is checked again when the report runs. Banners, titles and finding text from scanned hosts are escaped, and the renderer runs in its own process with no network or file access. Limits per report (500 hosts, 1,000 detailed findings, 500 hygiene findings) are in [Configuration](docs/CONFIGURATION.md#pdf-reports). Agents use `POST projects/{slug}/reports`, `GET .../reports/{id}` and the `export_report` and `get_report` tools ([API](docs/AGENT-API.md#pdf-reports)). The legacy per-scan report at `/scan/create_report/<id>` failed on every chart under WeasyPrint 70 (its resource fetcher used the interface WeasyPrint removed); it now renders, through the same closed fetcher the new reports use. The reports add `pypdf` (pinned, used by the tests) and the `fonts-dejavu-core` package to the image, and the software bill of materials now also lists the apt packages the Dockerfile installs by name. Needs migration 0098.
- **A slow UDP scan.** DNS, NTP, SNMP, IKE, IPMI, SSDP and memcached speak UDP, which the TCP sweeps never touched. A background sweep now scans 18 UDP ports that matter for exposure (`CVENGINE_UDPSCAN_PORTS` changes the list) with `nmap -sU -sV` at a low rate (one retry, 20 packets a second, version intensity 3), one IP at a time, each address once a week, on a queue of its own, on by default (`CVENGINE_UDPSCAN=0` turns it off), with the full port scan's scope, exclusion and pause rules and the bug bounty rule the other slow sweeps follow (a project whose engagement has not confirmed automated testing is skipped). Only a port that answered is stored as open; a silent one (`open|filtered`) is shown as a note on the host's Services tab and counts as nothing, so a firewall that drops UDP never inflates exposure or heat. Ports are now keyed by number and protocol (UDP 161 and TCP 161 are two rows), the TCP-only checks (nuclei, database, RDP and TLS) read TCP ports only, and `proto:udp` searches them. UDP services that name a product and version feed the same CVE correlation as TCP ones; memcached answering on UDP, SNMP answering the `public` community, NTP control queries, SSDP and IPMI become findings with a fix. A worker that cannot open raw sockets does not scan UDP and says why in the activity feed and the system status. See [Pipeline](docs/PIPELINE.md#keeping-scans-fast-and-polite) and [Configuration](docs/CONFIGURATION.md). New settings: `CVENGINE_UDPSCAN`, `CVENGINE_UDPSCAN_PORTS`, `CVENGINE_UDPSCAN_MAX_RATE`, `CVENGINE_UDPSCAN_HOST_TIMEOUT_MIN`, `CVENGINE_TTL_UDP_SCAN`. No migration.

### Changed

- **Scans run the whole Nuclei library by default.** The setting that limited scans to templates passing a read-only check (about half the community set) is now on the other way round: every installed template runs unless an App Admin turns **Settings, Scan engines, Nuclei templates** off. Nuclei's denial-of-service templates stay excluded, AI-written templates stay read-only, and destination checks still apply. Because validation uses the same setting, automatic and on-demand validation can now run templates that send requests other than plain reads. An update leaves an install where an App Admin chose a value as it is (the audit log records every change); an install nobody changed follows the new default.
- **x86-64 images no longer compile nmap.** On amd64 the image installs the nmap project's own release binary (checksum verified), which links its libraries in statically, so a build is minutes faster and runs the exact binary nmap publishes; arm64, for which nmap publishes no binary, still compiles the same release from source. Both end up in `/usr/local` and the build checks that nmap finds its scripts and data.
- **A CVE match now needs hard evidence, so a product name alone no longer produces findings.** A match is made only when the product on the host is the same product the CVE names and its version is inside a bounded affected range (or a template hit, a distribution tracker verdict, the host's own inventory report or runZero backs an existing match). A product seen without a version, for example a bare `nginx` or `next.js` fingerprint, used to become a candidate for every CVE that mentions the name (dozens per host); it now stays on the host as a technology or port and is listed as an **Unverified product** on the host page and in the host record (`unverified_products`), until a version is found. Advisories that give no usable version bound no longer match either. A new fire or smoke CVE is still template-tested on hosts that run its product with no version found, since a template hit is hard evidence without one.
- **Product names are matched as products, not as words.** `nginx` no longer matches `ingress-nginx`, `nginx-ui`, `nginx-proxy-manager` or the NGINX Ingress Controller, `cloudflare` no longer matches a WordPress plugin of that name, `jquery-ui-rails` is no longer read as jQuery UI, and a version is never compared with the ranges of another product. A shorter name still matches a longer one when the extra words only describe the same product (`Apache` and `Apache HTTP Server`, `Tomcat` and `Apache Tomcat`).
- **Operating-system and kernel flaws are host-only.** Linux kernel CVE records (including drivers such as ath11k, rtc, ksmbd), Windows kernel and component records without a network vector, and any CVE whose only product is an operating system are no longer matched from outside, and an OS fingerprint (`Ubuntu`, `Windows Server`, an nmap OS CPE) never matches a CVE by name. They stay decidable from a host's own inventory report. The classification of every stored CVE is redone automatically.
- **A CVE match now needs hard evidence, so a product name alone no longer produces findings.** A match is made only when the product on the host is the same product the CVE names and its version is inside a bounded affected range (or a template hit, a distribution tracker verdict, the host's own inventory report or runZero backs an existing match). A product seen without a version, for example a bare `nginx` or `next.js` fingerprint, used to become a candidate for every CVE that mentions the name (dozens per host); it now stays on the host as a technology or port and is listed as an **Unverified product** on the host page and in the host record (`unverified_products`), until a version is found. An advisory that gives no usable version bound no longer matches a versioned product either, with one deliberate exception: a CISA KEV advisory with no bound (Apache HTTP Server CVE-2021-41773, Jenkins CVE-2024-23897 and similar) is kept for a host running that product at a known version as a low-confidence candidate flagged `unbounded` that needs validation, never an "affected" verdict. A new fire or smoke CVE is template-tested on hosts that run its product with no version found, and on hosts whose version an unbounded advisory cannot judge, since a template hit is hard evidence without a version.
- **Product names are matched as products, not as words.** `nginx` no longer matches `ingress-nginx`, `nginx-ui`, `nginx-proxy-manager` or the NGINX Ingress Controller, `cloudflare` no longer matches a WordPress plugin of that name, `jquery-ui-rails` is no longer read as jQuery UI, and a version is never compared with the ranges of another product. A shorter name still matches a longer one when the extra words only describe the same product: descriptors (`Apache` and `Apache HTTP Server`), editions (`NGINX Open Source` and `NGINX Plus` are nginx, `Grafana OSS`, `GitLab CE/EE`, `Splunk Enterprise`, `Jira Software Data Center`, `Zimbra Collaboration Suite`, `Magento Open Source`, `Exchange Server 2016 Cumulative Update 14`), nmap service suffixes (`Postfix smtpd`, `Dovecot imapd`, `Samba smbd`, `Redis key-value store`), a vendor in front (`Fortinet FortiOS`, `Palo Alto Networks PAN-OS`, `MikroTik RouterOS`) and package or Maven coordinates (`openssl-libs`, `symfony/symfony`). A technology a scanner stored as a CPE (`cpe:/a:openbsd:openssh:7.2p2`) or a banner token (`OpenSSH_9.6p1 Ubuntu-3ubuntu13.5`) is read as its product and version.
- **Operating-system and kernel flaws are host-only.** Linux kernel CVE records with no network vector (the kernel CNA's driver fixes such as ath11k, rtc, ksmbd), Windows kernel and component records without a network vector, and any CVE whose only product is an operating system are no longer matched from outside, and an OS fingerprint (`Ubuntu`, `Windows Server`, an OS-only nmap CPE with no product name) never matches a CVE by name. A kernel flaw reachable over a network (CVE-2019-11477 SACK Panic, CVE-2022-0435 TIPC) and a kernel CVE on the CISA KEV list stay remote, and an application whose nmap CPE is only the system it runs on (`Microsoft HTTPAPI httpd`, `Linux telnetd`) is still an application. The classification of every stored CVE is redone automatically.
- **Version and identity probes: cvENGINE asks the host for the version it is missing.** A product seen without a version (nginx, Next.js, jQuery, an nmap service that states none) is a lead, and a probe now finds the missing fact, with no AI key needed. The probes are a catalogue of safe, read-only requests the application renders itself: the front page's headers and the modules an Apache `Server` header lists, the server's own error page (nginx, Apache, Tomcat, Jetty, IIS), the Next.js framework script, jQuery, Bootstrap and AngularJS versions, Drupal and Joomla version files, nginx-ui and Nginx Proxy Manager told apart from plain nginx, the Kubernetes default backend, and `nmap -sV --version-all` on one port with the safe banner scripts. What a probe finds is stored with its source and confidence (`ProbeObservation`, shown on the host page and in the host record as `probes`), written back as the host's technologies, and the host is correlated again, so the deterministic rules create a versioned match or none. A product that states no version is recorded as hidden, never guessed, and the AI is told so. Validation requests include the probes (a new `fact_probe` check kind) and no longer end with "no executable check" for a lead a probe can address; for a kernel or driver advisory the plan says the host inventory reporter is what decides. See [Pipeline](docs/PIPELINE.md#version-and-identity-probes). Needs migration 0088.
- **Version and identity probes: cvENGINE asks the host for the version it is missing.** A product seen without a version (nginx, Next.js, jQuery, an nmap service that states none) is a lead, and a probe now finds the missing fact, with no AI key needed. The probes are a catalogue of safe, read-only requests the application renders itself: the front page's headers and the modules an Apache `Server` header lists, the server's own error page (nginx, Apache, Tomcat, Jetty, IIS), the Next.js framework script, jQuery, Bootstrap and AngularJS versions, Drupal and Joomla version files, nginx-ui and Nginx Proxy Manager told apart from plain nginx, the Kubernetes default backend, and `nmap -sV --version-all` on one port with the safe banner scripts. What a probe finds is stored with its source and confidence (`ProbeObservation`, shown on the host page and in the host record as `probes`), written back as the host's technologies, and the host is correlated again, so the deterministic rules create a versioned match or none. A product that states no version is recorded as hidden, never guessed, and the AI is told so. Validation requests include the probes (a new `fact_probe` check kind) and no longer end with "no executable check" for a lead a probe can address; for a kernel or driver advisory the plan says the host inventory reporter is what decides. See [Pipeline](docs/PIPELINE.md#version-and-identity-probes). Needs migration 0091.
- **The AI judges a finding with everything cvENGINE holds about the host.** Triage and validation planning now receive the host's technologies with versions and sources, ports with product, version, extra info, banner, HTTP and TLS facts, the front page headers, CDN, WAF and hosting hints, certificates, the operating system guess and its runners-up, runZero and host-inventory facts, probe results, earlier checks and the host's other matches, cleaned, capped and marked untrusted, without host names. Behind a CDN or WAF the model is told that the Server header, X-Powered-By and error pages describe the edge, that a tag such as `cloudflare` names the edge and not an application, and to say so instead of assuming an origin. See [How the AI investigates a lead](docs/CVE-INTAKE.md#how-the-ai-investigates-a-lead).
- **WPScan runs on every host that shows any sign of WordPress, and says why when it did not.** Candidates come from a technology tag, a header or page signature, a `wp-login.php` form, plugin paths in crawled URLs, a title, the web archive's memory of the host, or an earlier probe. A read-only probe (login page, `/wp-json/` and its `Link` header, generator tag, readme) with browser-like request headers confirms it, follows apex and `www` redirects, and is run on every name that shares the host's address. Each candidate ends in a recorded state (scanned with when and how many findings, detected, blocked by the edge, failed with its reason, waiting, not WordPress) on the host page and in `get_system_status`. When an edge challenges the scanner (a Cloudflare challenge, a block page, a 202, 403, 429 or 503), the host says "the edge challenged the scanner" and what to allow in the WAF or bot rules; cvENGINE never tries to get past it. WPScan now works in two passes. The quick pass lists the plugins and themes the site's pages show, with versions and without an API token, and always finishes with a report; the thorough pass asks the site for every plugin and theme WPScan knows (hours per site) as a separate background task that never blocks the scan, is resumable and shows its state on the host page, keeps scanning without the token when its daily budget is spent (marking vulnerability data "not checked"), and a run that could not check it is repeated after a day while a clean run waits a week. Findings say what to do (update to the fixed version, remove the exposed file) and include outdated components, XML-RPC and timthumbs. Plugin and theme versions are also sightings: a plugin CVE matches only when the plugin's slug or readme title equals the product the advisory names and its version is inside the advisory's bounded range. See [Configuration](docs/CONFIGURATION.md) and [Pipeline](docs/PIPELINE.md#wordpress-and-wpscan). New settings: `CVENGINE_WP_PROBE_MAX_HOSTS`, `CVENGINE_PROBE_MAX_HOSTS`, `CVENGINE_TTL_PROBE`, `CVENGINE_TTL_WP_PROBE`, `CVENGINE_TTL_WPSCAN_PARTIAL`.

### Changed

- **An AI closure of a match needs concrete grounds.** A version inside an advisory's range is still a floor the model cannot clear, unless the application verified a ground from the host's metadata: a probe showed a look-alike product (plain nginx is not ingress-nginx or nginx-ui), or a CDN, WAF or hosting edge answers for the host on DNS-level evidence (never a response header the host controls) and the match rests on a web response. A match somebody vouches for is never cleared this way. Then the model's judgement stands; a banner that states no version is never a reason to close or to confirm. A new probe result re-asks the question without erasing the earlier validation record.
- **WPScan's enumeration and exit codes follow WPScan 4.** Plugins are no longer enumerated by default in WPScan 4, so the scan now asks for `ap,at,tt,cb,dbe,u1-10`. Exit code 4 (the scan did not finish) is read from the report's `scan_aborted` text as not WordPress, blocked by an edge, or a failure. A clean WPScan result stays fresh for a week (`CVENGINE_TTL_WPSCAN`, previously a day) and the per-scan site limit is 20.

- **BinaryEdge is retired.** Coalition shut BinaryEdge down as a standalone service on 31 March 2025 and bbot has no module for it, so the stored key could never be used. The API keys pages and the API now say so (`retired`, with the reason, and `used_in_scans: false`), no new BinaryEdge key is accepted, and a key saved earlier is kept, shown as retired and can be removed. The runZero key is now described as read by the scheduled sync rather than "not used yet".

### Fixed

- **Host assessments no longer trickle in one per project per three hours.** The three-hour AI review's `host_coverage_gap` offered the model at most one due host per project per window, so a project that picked up dozens of new subdomains from one discovery run (85 hosts, 83 never scanned, in one real case) drained at roughly one host every three hours, competing with triage, validation and discovery for the single model decision. A new, deterministic sweep (`cveFeed.coverage_topup`, task `top_up_host_assessments`, every 5 minutes, queue `discovery_queue`) tops up every project's host-assessment queue directly, with no model call, up to its free capacity (5 per project, 20 instance-wide), skipping a paused or bug-bounty-blocked project. `host_coverage_gap` is now a single-id view of the new `host_coverage_due(project, at, limit)`, which returns an ordered list of due host ids so the top-up sweep can take more than one per project per run; `assess_host` is no longer offered to the three-hour review's model (triage, validate and discover still are), though `dispatch`'s policy-checked `assess_host` entry point stays in place. `host_assessment.enqueue`'s existing active-row check and `IntegrityError` fallback already stop the review and the top-up from double-queuing the same host; a test now proves it. Needs migration 0104.
- **The new background sweeps respect the bug bounty gate.** The virtual host sweep, the subdirectory application sweep and the TLS audit now skip a project whose bug bounty engagement has not confirmed that automated testing is allowed, as the bucket and UDP sweeps and the work conductor already did.

- **A restart no longer leaves host assessments queued for a task that is gone, or blocks new ones.** The 30-minute re-queue now checks the broker and the workers (queues, unacknowledged messages, reserved, active and scheduled tasks) and leaves a row alone while its task still exists. A queued row whose task is gone no longer counts toward the 20 and 5 host-assessment caps, so "host assessment queue is at capacity" clears without waiting for the reaper. If the workers cannot be asked, nothing changes from before.
- **`get_system_status` no longer reports schedules as past due when beat itself is silent.** Beat writes a heartbeat every few seconds; the schedule list now carries `beat` (`running`, `silent` or `unknown`) and a beat silent for two minutes is reported as one problem ("celery beat has not ticked for N minutes") in place of every schedule being listed as late.
- **An update checks for room first and cleans up after itself.** Each update builds a new image of a few gigabytes and used to leave the replaced one behind, so a server that updated regularly slowly filled its disk and a later build failed half way, after the files were merged and scanning was paused. The updater now checks that there is room where Docker keeps its images (`CVENGINE_UPDATE_MIN_FREE_MB`, default 6144) and says so plainly instead of starting, and removes the replaced image once the update is verified.
- **An update no longer breaks the old containers while it is half applied.** Between the migration and the restart (and for as long as a failed restart is retried) the old code runs on the new schema. Seven columns added since v1.2.1 were `NOT NULL` with no database-level default, so the old code could not insert CVE records, detection records, host exclusions, project settings or API tokens in that time (the CVE feed sync failed at its next run). They now carry a database default (migrations `cveFeed` 0093 and `dashboard` 0029), and the upgrade rehearsal fails any release that adds such a column.
- **An update is not "done" until the web service answers.** After the restart the updater now waits for the web service to answer (a container restarting over and over is "running" to Docker) and checks that no migration is waiting; one that is gets applied, and if it still cannot be the update fails visibly and is retried instead of being reported as done. A failure at this point keeps the new code, as every failure after the migration does.
- **A finding marked fixed stays fixed when the next scan names its product differently.** Hosts are stored once per scan, so a new scan's match inherits the decision a person made about the earlier one, but only when the CVE, host, source and product string were all identical. A feed update that renamed the advisory's product, or the product seen on another port, produced a match under a new label that came back open (CVE-2018-16844 on the VPS). A decision now carries over by CVE and host when no match carries the same label, and the newest decision wins.
- **Queued work no longer waits twelve hours after an update restarts the stack.** Redis keeps a message a worker took and never started in its `unacked` list until the visibility timeout (12 hours here), so an update that killed the workers left host assessments, scans and validations "queued" for half a day while they counted toward the host-assessment limit ("host assessment queue is at capacity", with nothing running). The worker container now returns every orphaned message to its queue before its workers start (`manage.py requeue_unacked`), and the 15-minute reaper queues a host assessment again once it has waited 30 minutes, at most every two hours (a second message for a row that already started does nothing). The system status no longer reports a one-minute schedule as past due: beat records a task's last run only every three minutes, so a schedule is late only after that and a five-minute margin.
- **The Scan engines page did nothing, and every scan was labelled "Full Scan".** The editable YAML engines were inherited from reNgine and no part of the scan pipeline reads them; a scan runs one fixed pipeline whatever an engine says. All six inherited engines were also flagged default, so the first of them filed every scan, one-second host retests included, under "Full Scan". The editor and its create, update and delete endpoints are gone, as is the unused auto-fire engine setting. **Settings, Scan policy** keeps the one real setting that was on that page (let scans run every installed Nuclei template, not only the read-only ones), and an old `#engines` link opens it. Scans are filed under "CVEngine Recommended" and the scan detail no longer shows an "Engine" line. The engine rows, `list_engines` and `engine_id` on start scan stay, marked legacy, so existing agents keep working.
- **System status no longer reports healthy nuclei templates as stale, or a warning as the nuclei version.** The template files only change when a new release is published, so their age said nothing about whether the six-hourly refresh works (four quiet days read as "not refreshed for 87 hours" and marked the instance degraded). The refresh task now records when it last ran, whether it succeeded and the tool's last line; the status judges on that (a failed run, or no success for 36 hours, is the problem) and still shows when the files last changed. The nuclei version is the version line: a warning the tool prints first (nuclei does, on a Go release its JSON library has not caught up with) is skipped.
- **"Invalid product correlation closed" now says what happened.** The activity feed and the match detail called every automatic closure an "invalid product correlation", which reads as a parse failure and was also wrong for the local-only and no-version closures. The wording is now "different product", with the product the host was recorded as and the one the advisory covers (for example `"nginx"` against `"ingress-nginx"`), a one-line summary of the actual reason in the feed, and a badge on the detail page that says "different product", "no version evidence" or "local-only vulnerability" to match. The closures themselves are unchanged. Rows closed under the old wording are rewritten by a data migration (the host and product names in them are not stored, so those say "not one the advisory covers").
- **An update that was interrupted is no longer reported as done.** `web/.commit` is written when a deploy starts (the build needs it), so a run stopped after the files were merged but before the database migration left the new code, that marker and the old schema, and the next approval answered "already at" without deploying: the site then ran against a database that lacked the new tables and columns (MCP and the API returned 500). The updater now treats a deploy as finished only when a host-side record written after the services were recreated names the commit and `migrate --check` finds nothing waiting; otherwise it deploys again, and the log says why. An install that has not recorded a deploy yet rebuilds once on its next approval. To repair a host that is already in this state, run the build, `migrate`, `collectstatic` and recreate steps from `docs/UPDATES.md` ("When the files are new but the database is not").
- **The release gate fails closed.** `scripts/check_tool_versions.py --fail-if-behind` passed when a pin could not be found in its file (a renamed `ARG`) and when every version lookup failed. It now fails on a missing pin or an unreadable newest version too; the weekly report still shows such a row as `?`. The docs list honestly what is not pinned (base image digest, apt packages, transitive Python packages, GitHub Actions by commit, the moving service images), and a leftover `pip install tenacity` that ran at every container start (nothing uses it) is gone.
- **Agents: a busy agent no longer leaves work to a free one for ever.** An agent that heartbeats but never polls could hold a queued task indefinitely because the polling agent kept deferring to it; the deferral now lasts a minute.
- **MCP follows the specification's transport rules.** A browser page of another site (an `Origin` that is not this server's host) is refused, an unsupported `MCP-Protocol-Version` is a `400`, a client's own response is accepted with `202`, an unknown tool is the protocol error `-32602`, and an unexpected error is the JSON-RPC internal error `-32603` instead of an HTML page.
- **Host inventory.** A report that names a scanned host of the project is tied to it by that name; `CVENGINE_INVENTORY_STRICT=1` additionally requires an address in common with the host (a reported address, or the one it came from), otherwise the report waits for an administrator to adopt it. Held Debian and Ubuntu packages (`apt-mark hold`, often the stale ones) are now reported, and the Windows quick start downloads the script with `curl.exe` so a private CA is trusted.
- **Tailscale.** The tailnet-wide findings no longer stay on a device that left the tailnet: they move to another device and the old copies are resolved. The tailworm.com notes now say what Tailscale has actually said (acknowledged, fixes merged, no numbered release or bulletin yet as of 2026-10-27).
- **runZero import no longer adds addresses nobody authorized.** An inventory search such as `alive:true` can hold third-party public addresses, and import turned them into scan targets. A public address is now imported only inside a network the organization already has as a target (skipped as `not_owned` otherwise). Data from runZero no longer outlives it: an asset that leaves the inventory stops describing the host, and the confidence boost of a match is taken back when runZero stops reporting its CVE. A sync stops early, and says `quota_low`, when runZero reports the day's quota nearly spent.
- **Reasoning models work.** The verdict, validation plan and nuclei targeting parsers took the first `{` to the last `}`, so a reasoning model (qwen3 or deepseek-r1 served by LM Studio, Ollama or vLLM) that thinks in braces before answering produced no verdict. The thinking is now dropped and the last complete JSON object that has the expected keys is the answer.
- **The Netlas key is no longer on a command line.** WHOIS lookups used the `netlas` CLI with `-a <key>`, visible in `/proc/<pid>/cmdline`; they now use its Python client, which sends the key in a header.
- **Saved searches.** Changing a search's frequency now moves its next check (weekly to hourly no longer waits out the week), and an alert that no channel could deliver is tried again at the next check instead of being lost with the baseline.
- **AI server addresses.** Link-local and cloud metadata addresses are refused for every AI provider (local, LAN and tailnet servers stay allowed), and a failing model list names the kind of failure, not the address it tried.
- **Documentation and tests corrected.** runZero's migration number, token prefixes and the stale "not used yet" line; a note that Censys is retiring the legacy API bbot uses; a test that every service on the keys page feeds its key to its bbot module.
- **Existing false-positive matches close themselves, and never one somebody worked.** After each CVE update, the open matches that rest on a name alone (no version, no usable range, an OS fingerprint, a different product, or a local-only CVE) are closed with a stored reason ("Closed automatically: no version evidence ..."), the affected hosts are rescored, the dashboard counts refresh and the match's triage row reads "not applicable". A match is kept when anything vouches for it: a remediation state other than open, an assignee, a comment, a manual validation request, an AI "affected" verdict, a template hit, a distribution "vulnerable" verdict, a runZero report or a host report. A closure of a different-product match whose version is in range stays on that match and is never shared with other hosts. A closed match is not reopened by the next correlation unless new evidence (a version, or the KEV exception above) appears. The AI's correct "this is a different product" answer is no longer overruled by a "no supported evidence to close" note, but it cannot close a version inside the range or an end-of-life release older than every fixed branch.
- **Correlation over a large CVE catalogue is about seven times faster** in a synthetic 38,000-CVE benchmark: a product name is compared only with the products that share a word with it.
- **Versions present in tool output are no longer dropped or mangled.** An Apache `Server` header's module list (`PHP/5.4.16`, `OpenSSL/1.0.2k`, `mod_wsgi`) was ignored; `Apache-Coyote/1.1` was stored as Tomcat 1.1 (a protocol version, which then matched every old Tomcat advisory); `Jetty(9.4.51.v20230217)` was dropped; only the first generator meta tag of a WordPress page was read; the `X-AspNet-Version` header was never read; a CPE technology from Shodan's InternetDB and `Bootstrap:v3.3.7` were stored under names no advisory matches; a nuclei version with a `v` prefix was lost; a TLS web service on a port such as 8444 was asked over plain HTTP because nmap reports it as `http` with `tunnel="ssl"`; a version that nmap no longer reported (`server_tokens off`) stayed on the port; and the certificate and the runner-up operating system guesses nmap printed were not kept. nmap also now runs `http-server-header` and `http-generator`. Checked against real header and nmap XML samples.
- **A NUL byte in a request now counts as an attack, as intended.** The input guard rejected such a request but could not save its strike (the database refuses a NUL byte in text), so a flood of them never reached the automatic ban. The stored evidence is now cleaned first.
- **Leftovers of the retired tools removed.** The legacy API no longer offers a theHarvester config file (theHarvester was dropped earlier) and a stale amass heading is gone.
- **Outside-service API keys now reach bbot, and the default scan really enumerates subdomains.** Two defects hid each other. The keys were handed to bbot as `-c <file>`, but bbot 3.x accepts only `key=value` after `-c`: it printed a warning, ran no scan and exited 0, so any organization or project with a key stored got a discovery that "succeeded" with nothing found. And the default (standard) scan repeated `-p` once per preset, which bbot reads as "the last one wins", so it ran only the `web` preset and none of the subdomain sources, keyed or not. Keys now travel in a private preset file listed with the named presets after one `-p`, a run bbot refuses is a failure rather than an empty success, and the image build fails if bbot ever stops reading keys this way. Checked against bbot 3.0.2 (55 modules load, the keys appear in the merged preset).
- **A rejected API key is reported.** bbot skips a module whose key the service refuses and carries on, so a wrong key looked like a service that found nothing. An activity entry now names the service and the reason.
- **Censys, project keys and the vault, made truthful.** The Censys key was also mapped to `censys_ip`, which no scan mode enables; it is now only `censys_dns`, and the page says the key is the legacy Search API's ID and secret. A project key that holds only part of a pair no longer blanks the organization's. The install-wide Chaos key in the vault, which nothing read, is now the fallback for bbot's Chaos module; the unused OpenAI vault key is gone from the page (AI models are set under AI model).

- **An update now refreshes the proxy image, with nothing to run by hand.** The proxy (nginx) is a stock image, `nginx:alpine`, that was never re-pulled, so a host kept whatever nginx it first pulled for as long as it ran (one install was still on 1.29.4, behind the host's own nginx 1.24.0, which is also why the scanner saw two different nginx versions depending on whether it connected by address or by name). The proxy service now has `pull_policy: always`, so every `docker compose up` (the in-app updater, `update.sh`, `make up`) fetches the current image; with no network the update stops before anything is recreated and is retried.
- **An update carries its own deploy steps.** The in-app updater ran the deploy code it had already loaded, so a step added to an update arrived one update late. The steps now live in `scripts/update-deploy.sh`, which the updater reads after merging the new files, so an update applies its own steps in the same run (migrations were always applied automatically).
- **After an update, CVE guidance and local-only classification finish on their own.** The first CVE task after an update writes the rule-based guidance for every CVE (no AI cost), classifies local-only CVEs and closes their open matches, in batches within the task's time limit. Only the AI guidance spend stays opt-in, through the budget in Settings.
- **Local-only vulnerabilities are never matched to a host.** A kernel, driver or Win32k flaw (a "Windows Elevation of Privilege Vulnerability", a CVE with CVSS attack vector Local or Physical) is decided by software and patch levels an outside scan cannot see, so matching it to a host from a technology label such as "Windows Communication Foundation" was always a false positive (and the validation requests it started could only end "cannot be established"). Each CVE is now classified `local_only` from its CVSS vector, or, when it has none, from its wording and affected product; such CVEs leave the correlation pool, their open matches are closed ("Closed: local-only vulnerability", the host rescored; work a person did is kept) and they spend no AI budget. Needs migration 0083.
- **"Remove & exclude" now removes the host you see.** The Hosts page shows names that point at the same addresses as one host, so excluding only the name on display removed that one name and the next name took its place: the host seemed not to go however often you clicked. The dialog now offers to remove and exclude those other names too (on by default, with the names listed), the same for **Delete host data**, and a failure shows inside the dialog instead of a toast that is gone in seconds. `exclude_host` and `delete_host` take `with_aliases`.
- **A finding marked fixed (or an accepted risk) no longer keeps its host hot.** The host's colour, score, "why this host is fire" list, the exposed counts and the graph only skipped CVE matches the AI had ruled out; a match or scanner finding someone had closed still counted, so a host with every finding closed stayed "fire". Closed findings now drop out of all of them, and marking a finding fixed, accepted, reopened or in progress rescores its host at once (a bulk update rescores each host once) instead of at the next daily pass. A host closed before this change is corrected by the next rescore.

### Added

- **Archive, restore, pin or unpin many hosts at once.** `POST projects/{slug}/hosts/bulk` and the MCP tool `bulk_host_action` take up to 200 host names and one action. Each host is independent: a pinned host that cannot be archived, or a name that is not in the project, is listed in `failed` and the rest are done.
- **LM Studio as an AI model.** Settings, AI model now offers LM Studio beside Ollama and vLLM: enter its address (port 1234, `/v1`), load the models it holds (embedding models are left out), and optionally the API token its authentication needs. It speaks the OpenAI-compatible API, so triage, validation planning, CVE guidance and screenshot reading (with a vision model) all work, and a model that has to load on its first request gets five minutes instead of two. See [Deployment](docs/DEPLOYMENT.md#ai-models).
- **Saved searches with alerts.** Type a search on the Hosts page, choose **Save search**, and cvENGINE re-runs it every hour, day or week with your own access and tells Slack, Discord, Telegram or Lark when a new host starts to match (or, if you ask, when hosts stop matching or the count reaches a number). The first check only records what matches now, so nothing floods your channel; after that you get one message per search per check, naming up to five new hosts, with a link, plus a line in the Activity feed. Names on the same addresses count as one host. Searches are private to you and one project (25 per person, 100 per project); a search is checked with the same code as the search box and refused at save time if it would not run. If you leave the project the search is switched off, not run. The Saved searches list can run, edit, switch off or delete a search and import the views kept in your browser, which keep working. Over the API: `projects/{slug}/saved-searches` and the MCP tools `list_saved_searches`, `create_saved_search`, `update_saved_search`, `delete_saved_search`, `run_saved_search` and `get_saved_search_matches`. See [docs/SEARCH.md](docs/SEARCH.md). Needs migrations 0085 and 0086 (cveFeed).
- **runZero asset-inventory sync.** Save a runZero Export API token under Settings, API keys, and cvENGINE reads your runZero inventory, read-only, every 6 hours (`CVENGINE_RUNZERO_SYNC_HOURS`) and on **Sync now**. Hosts cvENGINE already holds get runZero's operating system, hardware, type, tags, site and names (shown on the host page and in `ips[].runzero`), and runZero's own CVE reports for them are listed as **runZero reports**: extra evidence that raises the confidence of a CVE match cvENGINE already found on the same host and port, never a new match and never a confirmation. A per-project switch (off by default) also imports the addresses runZero knows and cvENGINE does not as targets, through the same checks as Add targets (exclusions, private ranges, organization uniqueness), limited by a runZero search (default `alive:true`) and at most 500 per sync (`CVENGINE_RUNZERO_IMPORT_MAX`), with the skipped ones counted by reason. The API keys page shows the last sync, its counts and any error; a bad token, a rate limit or an unreachable console is reported in words and retried at the next sync. REST `GET/PUT projects/{slug}/runzero` and `POST .../runzero/sync`, MCP `get_runzero_sync`, `set_runzero_sync` and `run_runzero_sync` (organization admin or App Admin), and `get_system_status` lists each project's sync. Only the hosted console (`console.runzero.com`) is supported. See [Configuration](docs/CONFIGURATION.md#runzero). Needs migration 0085.
- **Attach a Tailscale tailnet and scan it.** Settings > Tailscale connects a tailnet to a project: its devices become targets automatically (named by their tailnet address, with the MagicDNS name, OS, client version, tags, owner, key expiry, routes and online state kept with them), offline devices are marked, devices that leave are archived and come back if they return, and devices shared in from another tailnet, this server's own node and anything an organization excluded are never imported. With auto-scan on, new and changed online devices go through the normal discovery, nmap and nuclei pipeline, up to a per-run cap; the tag and operating-system filters, the cap and a **Test connection** button are on the same page, with the tailnet name, device counts, last sync and last error. A sync runs every 15 minutes (or **Sync now**). Credentials (a read-only OAuth client or an API access token) are stored encrypted and never shown again. Scans reach the tailnet through an optional `tailscale` compose profile (`COMPOSE_PROFILES=tailscale`, the auth key in `.env.tailscale`) that joins the scanner's network namespace; without it the stack is unchanged, and an install whose Docker host is already on the tailnet can choose "Already routed". An address in `100.64.0.0/10` is scannable only for the project that owns that imported device, only while the link has synced successfully in the last 30 minutes and (with the compose profile) the scanner's node is up, and without `CVENGINE_ALLOW_PRIVATE_TARGETS`; a name that resolves into a tailnet, an address typed by hand and every other project are still refused. The node runs with `--shields-up`, a pinned image and no control socket outside it (the worker reads a status file the sidecar publishes), and a sync by hand is limited to one a minute. See [docs/TAILSCALE.md](docs/TAILSCALE.md). Needs migrations 0088 and 0089.
- **Tailscale findings.** Each sync checks the tailnet and its devices and stores hygiene findings with the usual remediation workflow: a client older than a [Tailscale security bulletin](https://tailscale.com/security-bulletins)'s fixed version (only where the feature the bulletin needs is present), a pending client update, key expiry disabled on a user-owned device, a device unseen for 90 days, approved subnet routes of /16 or wider, exit nodes, remote-administration ports found open on a tailnet address, and from the policy file an allow-all rule, broad or root Tailscale SSH accept rules, tag owners that name every member and a Funnel entitlement for every member. Each finding has its severity reason, evidence, a fix and a stable key.
